Every step cites the Comprehensive Rules, and an adversarial checker verified each citation against the full rule text. The complete case files are in Judge's Desk.
◆ DATA-DERIVED
Numbers from a seeded, reproducible simulation committed to the repository. Same seed, same answer, every time.
★ COACHING
Judgment — grounded in the verified lines and the numbers, but a human call, and labeled as one.

The Command Zone

by Counselor Vera Dictum with Coach Sunny Brightside
✓ RULES-VERIFIED★ COACHING
Why this commander is exactly where you want to be — on the record.
THE ZONE

TRADE HIM. THEN PROTECT HIM.

One card in this deck is exempt from variance, and a single rule says so. Three of your six verified kills need him standing and the other three never say his name — so when do you spend the guarantee, and when do you spend the insurance?

Art: Mark Winters
Dominaria Remastered #110
CastPrinted costCommander taxTotal mana
1st cast{2}{B}{B}+{0}4
2nd cast{2}{B}{B}+{2}6
3rd cast{2}{B}{B}+{4}8
4th cast{2}{B}{B}+{6}10
Commander File
Commander
Yawgmoth, Thran Physician
Mana cost
{2}{B}{B}
Color identity
B
Type
Legendary Creature — Human Cleric
Mean cast turn
4.301
Out by turn 6
91%

Begin with the clause that makes this format unlike every other one. CR 903.8: "A player may cast a commander they own from the command zone." Yawgmoth is therefore not a card you draw. He is a card you have. The simulation casts him in 9,850 of 10,000 games, mean turn 4.301, median 4, and 7,188 of those land him on turn four exactly — 91% are on the battlefield by turn six, and the 718 turn-two arrivals every one ride Sol Ring, Ancient Tomb or Dark Ritual.

The price of the guarantee sits in the same rule, and it compounds: "an additional {2} for each previous time the player casting it has cast it from the command zone that game." Four mana, then six, then eight, then ten. Set that schedule beside mean available mana of 3.908 on turn four, 5.230 on turn six and 7.104 on turn ten. The holding is short: the first recast costs you a turn, and the third costs a turn nobody at this table is going to give you. Notice, though, which of your saves are not recasts at all — Malakir Rebirth and Supernatural Stamina return him to the battlefield or keep him there, so the tax never advances.

Colour identity is CR 903.4 — "the color or colors of any mana symbols in that card's mana cost or rules text". One black symbol in his cost, one colour in the deck, and the mana audit has nothing to reconcile: black is 100% of the pip load and 100% of the sources, on curve at 94.8% once the rocks and dorks are counted. What that identity costs is breadth, and this issue keeps the bill in view: no counterspell, and not one card among all 80 entries that destroys or exiles an artifact. Five tutors cannot find what is not there.

Commander damage the record disposes of quickly. CR 903.10a removes a player dealt 21 or more combat damage by the same commander. Yawgmoth is a 2/4 who would much rather be sacrificing than attacking; 21 damage is eleven connections. A coincidence here, never a clock.

Now the clause to memorise, because it cuts both ways. CR 702.16b: a permanent with protection cannot be targeted by abilities from a source with the stated quality. Yawgmoth is a Human with protection from Humans, so his own ability can never target him — the 2/4 who could comfortably absorb counters is not allowed to take one. The Counselor's files lean on this: once an opponent's creature is gone, every legal target is one of your own bodies, which is exactly what bounds a Nest rotation at five to eight activations. In the other direction it is free armour at a table thick with Human commanders.

One last fact about the zone: four players can see it, and they cannot see your hand. What the table prices when this commander resolves is a reputation. The bracket sheet agrees with them — floor 4, Optimized, forced independently by four Game Changers and by eleven two-card infinite records, inside a declared target of 4.

COACH'S READ. The rule is phase, not posture. Three of your six signed kills never name him — both Gravecrawler loops and the Exquisite Blood pair. Three gate on him, including the primary Nest engine and both counted undying lines. So in the grind, trade him cheerfully: he is a draw engine and a removal magnet, the recast is two mana and a turn, and removal spent on him is removal not spent on your drains. On the engine turn he is the hub, and exactly three cards can carry him through a removal spell — Lightning Greaves, Supernatural Stamina, Malakir Rebirth. Those are engine-turn cards. Never spend one for value, and never mulligan toward a commander who arrives on his own in seven games of ten.

Pilot TipThe best reason to keep him on the battlefield is this card — free removal that exiles, on any turn you spent assembling.
The Command ZoneMANA MAP · VOL. 008

The Game Plan

by Coach Sunny Brightside
★ COACHING
What this deck wants to do — and why it's going to work.
THE PLAN

A VALUE DECK UNTIL IT ISN'T

The table sees a swarm of one-mana bodies and a doctor drawing cards. What they are looking at is six verified ways to end all of them at once — you just haven't picked one yet.

The table will look at the commander, decide you are the Yawgmoth deck, and point their removal at him. Half the time, let them. He recasts from the command zone, his job in the grind is cards — that ability costs no mana at all, so it runs through a tax, through a lock, and at instant speed on somebody else's end step — and he arrives on turn four in 7,188 of 10,000 goldfished games without any help from you. But know which kill you are on before you shrug. The two Gravecrawler loops and the Exquisite Blood pair never name him; the Nest engine, the Mikaeus line and the undying pair all need him standing. Exactly three cards carry him through a removal spell — Lightning Greaves, Supernatural Stamina, Malakir Rebirth. Those are engine-turn cards. Never spend one for value.

You have six verified shapes, and every one of them ends the whole table at once — so never aim a kill at a seat. Two are unbounded: stack 009 funds itself with Treasure, and stack 011 pays nothing at all and gains you a life a lap. Stack 012 is lethal precisely because it cannot stop. Two spend your library, and spend it with room to spare: stack 010 needs 39 activations of the 59 you get, stack 014 kills in about 30 of the roughly 55 available. And the sixth is the one you will actually play most: Nest of Scarabs plus any drain, rotations stacked across turns, Yawgmoth doing the counting.

Here is the gap you pilot around. The enabler layers are enormous — by turn 10 you have drawn a drain 82.3% of the time, renewable fodder 82.3%, a free sacrifice outlet 78.5%, an ignition source 73.8%. The named kills read tiny beside that: 13.5% for the Nest engine unassisted, single digits for everything else. Read those one at a time and never add them up. The machine will be running; your turns go to hunting the one missing card. That is what five tutors are for — a tutor is what carries the Exquisite Blood kill from 6.6% to 42.6% — so never cast one for value either.

A wrath is not what beats you. Bastion of Remembrance, Zulaport Cutthroat, Blood Artist and The Meathook Massacre all get paid when your creatures die, and Gravecrawler and Reassembling Skeleton rebuild cheaper than anyone at the table. What beats you is a permanent you cannot remove: one card in the 99 destroys a noncreature permanent an opponent controls, and it only reaches enchantments. So stop planning to answer the hate and pick the kill that dodges it — Exquisite Blood needs no graveyard, no outlet, no recursion and no commander. Deploy in the order that costs you least when it gets answered, put the drain down last, and say bracket 4 at Rule 0 — four Game Changers and eleven two-card infinite records force it — then go play exactly the deck you just described.

1
BUY CHEAP, BUY EARLYYour enabler classes arrive first and they arrive on their own: renewable fodder at a mean draw turn of 2.578, a drain at 2.582, a free repeatable outlet at 2.690, an ignition source at 2.818. Carrion Feeder, Viscera Seer, Fume Spitter, Bitterblossom and Reassembling Skeleton all cost one or two mana. Put them down and stop admiring them.
2
LAND HIM ON CURVE, THEN DIGThe commander lands on turn four in 7,188 of 10,000 goldfished games and is on the battlefield by turn six in 91%. From that turn every spare body is a card, at no mana, at instant speed, on other people's turns. One honest note about every number in this issue: the goldfish does not model his draw, so each unassisted rate is a floor on games where he sticks and digs.
3
HOLD THE DRAINYou hold some drain by turn ten in 82.3% of games, and by turn six in 72.0%. Bastion of Remembrance specifically — the drain five signed files resolved with — is one card at 17.8%. A drain in hand is a surprise; a drain on the battlefield is a target. It is the piece that reveals the plan, so it goes down last, at the end step before your own turn.
4
NAME THE SHAPE BEFORE YOU GOThere are exactly six. Two unbounded loops — decisions you make once. One mandatory pair that wins because it cannot stop. Two library-bounded lines — arithmetic you count at the table. And the primary: Nest of Scarabs plus any of nine drains, a pace you stack across turns. Every one ends all opponents at the same check, so never aim a kill at a seat. Ask instead which shape you are one card away from.
Pilot TipPlay it on turn one and let the table file it as a body — the tell in this deck is never Gravecrawler, it is whatever you play beside it.
Pilot TipCast it on turn two every single time — a body every upkeep for 1 life is the cheapest fodder you will ever buy.
You are nothing, and then you are the whole table. There is no board in between.
The Game PlanMANA MAP · VOL. 008

The 99

by Coach Sunny Brightside
★ COACHING
Roll call. Every card earns its seat — or hears about it.
ROLL CALL

ELEVEN GROUPS, AND THE ONE THAT ISN'T HERE

Read the first group and you know how this deck ends games. Read to the end and you will notice the group nobody could write for it: the cards that destroy an artifact.

The six kills — the cards that end the table

Gravecrawler

Mill + Graveyard

The one slot with no substitute — two unbounded kills route through him. Keep a second Zombie on the battlefield at all times so his recast permission stays live, and never let the loop eat that Zombie.

Bastion of Remembrance

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

Treat this as a combo piece, not a value enchantment — it is the drain three of your verified kills route through, so resolve it on the turn you mean to convert rather than the turn you draw it. It also hands you a free Soldier to eat on arrival.

Warren Soultrader

Attack Trigger + TokensCounters + Tokens

A Zombie who turns each sacrifice into a Treasure, and that Treasure exactly pays Gravecrawler's next cast. He satisfies the Zombie condition himself, so sacrifice anything on the board except him.

Phyrexian Altar

Sac + Death TriggerSac + ETB

Your best engine mana: sacrifice for any colour, and it never uses the stack, so nobody gets a window to respond. With Gravecrawler and a spare Zombie the loop costs you nothing to run.

Exquisite Blood

Your fastest kill and the only one that touches no graveyard, no outlet and no commander, so reach for this shape the moment somebody lands hate on the rest of the deck. The pair comes together on a mean turn 5.1, so play toward it early rather than treating it as a late-game plan.

South Wind Avatar

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

Deathtouch keeps him alive on defence and his toughness soaks counters, but the line that matters is the second one — gain a life, every opponent loses one. Put him beside Exquisite Blood and stop looking for anything else.

Marauding Blight-Priest

Lifegain + Death Trigger

The same each-opponent lifegain trigger on a cheaper body — your second and third shot at the Exquisite Blood kill. Deploy him early, while the table still reads him as a 2/2 that does nothing.

Starscape Cleric

Flying + Damage TriggerLifegain + Death Trigger

Cheap, flies, and carries the lifegain drain. Buy the Offspring copy whenever the mana is spare: two bodies with that trigger means the table loses two life for every life you gain.

Mikaeus, the Unhallowed

Flying + Damage TriggerLifegain + Death Trigger

Six mana turns every non-Human you own into a body that comes back, and beside the commander that alternation is a kill. Cast him when you can still hold something up, because the loop itself asks for no more mana.

Nest of Scarabs

Every counter you place becomes a body, so point the commander's counters at their creatures when you need the board clear and at your own when you need fodder. Count by creatures touched, not counters placed — one proliferate across three counter-bearing creatures hands you three Insects.

Butcher Ghoul

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

Half of the verified undying pair: under the commander he and Geralf's Messenger alternate deaths for as long as your library holds out — stack 014's kill in about 30 activations. On his own he is still a Zombie that dies twice and holds Gravecrawler's recast permission open.

Geralf's Messenger

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

The other half of stack 014's pair, and the seat his enters-trigger points at loses double what the rest of the table does — aim it at whoever is closest to dead. Three black pips and he enters tapped, so cast him on a turn where a tapped body costs you nothing.

The other drains — each opponent, or one

Zulaport Cutthroat

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

A drain on a body that slots into any recursion kill, and nothing can force you to eat him. Sacrifice him last and on purpose: his own trigger sees his death and banks one more drain.

Ayara, First of Locthwain

Draw + DiscardLifegain + Death Trigger

Play her the moment your first three lands are black and let every black creature that follows chip the whole table. Keep one spare black body back for her tap-to-draw rather than feeding everything to the commander.

The Meathook Massacre

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

Cast it when your board is the one that profits — your creatures dying drains each opponent, theirs dying gains you life, and X scales to whatever has been deployed. This is the wipe you want to be holding, not the one you fear.

Mirkwood Bats

Flying + Damage TriggerSac + Death Trigger

Get it down before you start converting tokens, not after: one commander activation paid with a token pays this drain twice, once going out and once coming back. Its 2/3 body also absorbs two counters and lives, which buys you activations a 1/1 cannot.

Blood Artist

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

He hits one seat per trigger where your real finishers hit every opponent at once, so play him for value and never tutor for him as the kill. He is still the best thing to have on the battlefield when somebody wraths.

Gray Merchant of Asphodel

Blink + ETBLifegain + Death Trigger

Count your devotion before you cast him and pick the turn the table is already low — in a list this full of black pips the number gets ugly fast. He is a Zombie for Gravecrawler and a reanimation target besides.

Free sacrifice outlets — 78.5% by turn ten

Carrion Feeder

Counters + TokensSac + Death Trigger

One mana, a free outlet, and a Zombie — that last word is why he can stand in the recursion loop and hold Gravecrawler's permission open. He cannot block, so plan your defence elsewhere.

Viscera Seer

Sac + Death TriggerSac + ETB

One mana for a free outlet that filters every draw. Keep her out of the Mikaeus loop mid-stream — her sacrifice places no counter, so it costs a body you cannot re-arm. Save her for the finish.

Woe Strider

Attack Trigger + TokensCounters + Tokens

A free outlet, a Goat to feed it on arrival, and escape from your own graveyard after the table wipes. Three jobs for three mana, and you will use all three.

Yahenni, Undying Partisan

Counters + TokensLifegain + Death Trigger

The outlet that lives through your own sweepers — sacrifice anything and she is indestructible for the turn. She is also a legal undying body under Mikaeus.

Blasting Station

Sac + Death TriggerSac + ETB

A free outlet that closes from a board no drain can reach, and it untaps every time a creature enters, which with your token production is constantly. Sequence your tokens into it and keep pulling the trigger.

Ashnod's Altar

Sac + Death TriggerSac + ETB

Free outlet and two colorless whenever you need a body gone at instant speed. Know the limit before you rely on it: colorless does not recast Gravecrawler, so use it for outlet work and bulk mana, never as the loop's fuel.

Phyrexian Tower

Sac + Death TriggerSac + ETB

An outlet, two black mana and a land drop in one slot. Save the activation for the turn the mana matters — you only get it once a turn.

Renewable fodder — the bodies that come back

Reassembling Skeleton

Mill + GraveyardReanimate + Death Trigger

Pay two, get a body, forever. Hold the mana for it on any turn you have nothing better to do — this is the fodder that never runs out.

Bitterblossom

Flying + Damage Trigger

Two mana for a body every upkeep, at a life apiece, for the rest of the game — and it keeps producing while you hold up interaction. Play it on turn two every single time.

Ophiomancer

Attack Trigger + TokensCounters + Tokens

A deathtouch Snake at the beginning of every upkeep — yours and theirs — so long as the last one is gone. Feed each Snake to an outlet before the next upkeep and one two-drop hands you a body per player per rotation, forever.

Pawn of Ulamog

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

Every nontoken creature you lose banks a Spawn that is both one more body to spend and a sacrifice-for-{C}. Know the limit: colorless pays the generic half of a big turn, never Gravecrawler's {B} — spend the Spawns on casting, and then spend the Spawns.

Stitcher's Supplier

Lifegain + Death TriggerMill + Graveyard

One mana for a Zombie that fills your graveyard coming in and going out. Play it on turn one — your recursion suite wants targets more than you want the 1/1.

Sedgemoor Witch

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

Ward makes her expensive to answer and every instant or sorcery you cast hands you a Pest. Those Pests gain you life when they die, which your lifegain drains are watching for.

Grave Titan

Attack Trigger + TokensCounters + Tokens

Ten power across three bodies, and two more Zombies every time it attacks. This is how an emptied board becomes an engine again, and it is the best thing in your graveyard to reanimate.

Black Market Connections

Attack Trigger + TokensCounters + Tokens

Take the mode the board is short of every first main phase — a Treasure, a card, or a 3/2 — and pay the life without flinching. That flexibility beats a fixed draw engine in a deck that needs a different resource every turn.

Counters, and what they make

Fume Spitter

-1/-1 + Death Trigger

One mana, sacrifices itself, places a counter — the cheapest ignition you own and a death trigger in the same card. Play it turn one when the alternative is nothing.

Skinrender

-1/-1 + Death TriggerBlink + ETB

Three counters on arrival: kill their creature outright, or aim it at your own body and collect three Insects. Choose by what the board is short of, and it is usually bodies.

Soul Snuffers

-1/-1 + Death TriggerBlink + ETB

A counter on every creature sweeps the small stuff and lights the Nest across your whole board in one cast. Yes, it hits yours — that is normally why you are casting it.

Sinister Gnarlbark

-1/-1 + Death TriggerCounterspell + Draw

A card and a free counter at every end step, which with the Nest out is a free Insect every turn as well. Three mana for ignition you never have to pay for again.

Massacre Girl, Known Killer

Draw + DiscardLifegain + Death Trigger

Wither makes your combat damage into counters, which is removal, ignition and Nest fuel from one attack step. Every small creature an opponent loses draws you a card on top of it.

Mana that is not a land

Sol Ring

Ramp + Cost Reduction

Two colorless on turn one is why your commander sometimes arrives on turn two. Play it the moment you see it and never think about it again.

Dark Ritual

Ramp + Cost Reduction

One black into three, once. Spend it on the turn that changes the game — a turn-one Bitterblossom, a turn-two commander — and never on a turn you were merely ahead anyway.

Jet Medallion

Ramp + Cost ReductionStorm + Cost Reduction

One generic off every black spell you cast, which in a deck this cheap means most turns play one spell deeper. It discounts casts, not activations — the commander's ability was already free.

Pitiless Plunderer

Attack Trigger + TokensCounters + Tokens

Every other creature you lose becomes a Treasure, which turns a board wipe into a pile of mana. Four mana looks steep right up until the turn you sacrifice five things.

Draw, priced in life

Necropotence

Draw + DiscardMill + Graveyard

Life is your cheapest resource in a deck whose loops finish at 80, so spend it hard. Decide how many you want before you start paying: the cards arrive at your end step, and a turn spent overpaying is a turn you cannot cast them.

Skullclamp

Draw + DiscardEquipment + Attack Trigger

One mana, and every 1/1 you own becomes two cards. With Bitterblossom, Nest of Scarabs and Grave Titan all making bodies, equip it the moment you have a token you are happy to kill.

Deadly Dispute

Attack Trigger + TokensCounters + Tokens

Instant-speed conversion: a creature already in the removal crosshairs becomes two cards and a Treasure. Cast it in response to the removal, never before.

Plumb the Forbidden

Counterspell + DrawDraw + Discard

Your insurance against a wrath or a targeted exile — sacrifice as many creatures as you like in response and copy it for each. Keep two mana up any turn a sweeper is live.

The five wishes

Demonic Tutor

Two mana, any card, straight to hand. When a kill is one piece short this is the shortest road there — do not spend it on a land.

Vampiric Tutor

One mana at instant speed for the exact card you need, on top of your library. Cast it at the end of the turn before you need it, because it costs you a draw step.

Diabolic Intent

Sac + Death TriggerSac + ETB

The additional cost is a creature dying, which every payoff on your board is already waiting for. Sacrifice into your drains and you get the tutor at a discount you were collecting anyway.

Beseech the Mirror

Sac + Death TriggerSac + ETB

Bargain something you were going to sacrifice anyway and the four-or-less piece arrives free. Hold this one for the turn you want the tutor and the kill to happen together.

Insatiable Avarice

Three mana either way — one mode sets the top of your library, the other draws three for 3 life — and five mana buys both on one cast. Take the tutor mode when you are a piece short and both when you are short and out of cards.

Interaction — and where it stops

Feed the Swarm

Removal + Death Trigger

The one card in the 99 that destroys a noncreature permanent an opponent controls, and it only reaches enchantments. Hold it for the permanent that genuinely stops you rather than the first irritating thing you see.

Deadly Rollick

Removal + Death Trigger

Free while your commander is on the battlefield, and it exiles rather than destroys. This is the best argument for keeping Yawgmoth around on the turns you are not using him.

Fatal Push

Removal + Death Trigger

One mana kills a two-drop, or a four-drop the moment anything of yours has left the battlefield this turn. In this deck that condition is on every turn — sacrifice something first and swing the ceiling up.

Black Sun's Zenith

The only sweeper you have that leaves counters behind, which makes it the only sweeper the Nest can see. Set X to what kills their board and let your drains collect on yours.

Grave Pact

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

Your only repeatable edict and your answer to a creature you cannot target: every sacrifice you were making anyway now costs each opponent a body. Land it before the protected threat arrives, not after.

The rebuild package

Victimize

Mill + GraveyardSac + Death Trigger

One sacrifice, two bodies back tapped. It pays every layer at once: a death trigger going out and two arrivals coming in.

Persist

Mill + GraveyardReanimate + Death Trigger

Two mana returns a nonlegendary body carrying a counter — a creature and an Insect on one card when the Nest is out. Point it at something worth more than the counter takes off it.

Animate Dead

Aura + ProtectionBounce + ETB

Two mana for the best creature in any graveyard, theirs included. The -1/-0 never matters when the body's job is to die again.

Phyrexian Reclamation

Mill + Graveyard

Two mana and 2 life, at instant speed, as often as you like — this is how you rebuild through hate aimed at the battlefield. Buy the answered piece back on their end step.

Supernatural Stamina

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

One mana saves a creature from removal and hands you a free death trigger on the way through. Keep it up on any turn your drain is out and the table has mana open.

Malakir Rebirth // Malakir Mire

Lifegain + Death TriggerReanimate + Death Trigger

A land on the turns you are short and a two-life save on the turn your key creature gets targeted. The return is another death trigger for every drain you have out.

Lightning Greaves

Aura + ProtectionEquipment + Attack Trigger

Zero equip pulls a piece out of the crosshairs and lets an outlet work the turn it lands. Move the boots off before you need to target that creature yourself.

The mana — 36 copies, one colour

Swamp

Ramp + Cost Reduction

Twenty-one of them, which is why the black count works and why Cabal Coffers does what it does. Lead on a basic and keep the utility lands for turns you can afford to spend on them.

Urborg, Tomb of Yawgmoth

It makes no mana itself and counts for nothing in the source column — what it does is turn every land you own into a Swamp for Cabal Coffers. Get it down early and let the rest of the base improve.

Cabal Coffers

Ramp + Cost Reduction

Two mana and a tap for a black per Swamp, which with 21 basics and Urborg out is the biggest tap you will make all game. It counts as one source on paper; play it like several.

Nykthos, Shrine to Nyx

Ramp + Cost Reduction

Two mana and a tap for your devotion to black, and in a list where nearly every permanent carries black pips the number climbs fast. On paper it counts for nothing; in play it is the biggest single burst of {B} the base can make — fire it on the turn you mean to dump your hand, not to fix a curve.

Ancient Tomb

Ramp + Cost Reduction

Two mana out of a land for 2 damage a tap. Colorless is a genuine constraint here, so spend it on the generic half of a cost and leave the pips to a Swamp.

Cabal Pit

Mill + GraveyardRamp + Cost Reduction

Black mana for a point of damage, and once your graveyard is seven deep it sacrifices itself to shrink a creature. Hold that mode for a blocker you actually need gone.

Ifnir Deadlands

-1/-1 + Death TriggerSac + Death Trigger

Black mana for a life, plus a late-game sink that drops two counters on an opposing creature. That is removal and Nest ignition out of a land slot — keep the Desert around to pay for it.

Castle Locthwain

Counterspell + DrawDraw + Discard

Untapped black as long as you control a Swamp, which here is nearly always. Its draw costs life equal to your hand size, so fire it when your hand is almost empty.

Takenuma, Abandoned Mire

Mill + GraveyardRamp + Cost Reduction

Black mana, or channel it away to buy a creature back — cheaper for every legendary creature you control, and you run five. A land that doubles as a recursion spell.

Bojuka Bog

Blink + ETBReanimate + ETB

A tapped land that exiles a graveyard on arrival. Hold it for the reanimator seat instead of jamming it turn one; a tapped land costs you almost nothing on turn six.

Command Tower

Ramp + Cost Reduction

Untapped black, no condition, no cost. Nothing here to think about.

City of Brass

Ramp + Cost Reduction

Any colour, untapped, a point of damage a tap. In a deck whose kills finish at 80 life you will pay that all game and smile.

Exotic Orchard

Ramp + Cost Reduction

It taps for whatever an opponent's lands could make, which is black often enough to count on. Glance at the table before you build a triple-black turn around it.

Survivors' Encampment

Ramp + Cost Reduction

Colorless, or any colour by tapping a spare body, and you generally have one. Tap the creature you were not planning to sacrifice this turn.

Path of Ancestry

Ramp + Cost Reduction

Enters tapped for black, and scries whenever that mana casts a Human or a Cleric. Put it down on a turn you were not going to spend all your mana anyway — with only three taplands in the base, that turn always comes.

Agadeem's Awakening // Agadeem, the Undercrypt

Mill + GraveyardRamp + Cost Reduction

Pay the 3 life to have the land side arrive untapped; here that is almost always right. Keep the front face in mind for the turn after a wipe, when it brings your whole curve back at once.

1
TWO CARDS CARRY THE LOOPSGravecrawler is the recursive half of both unbounded kills; Bastion of Remembrance is the drain five signed files resolved with. Each is one card, about 7% of an opening seven, drawn by turn ten in 17.0% and 17.8% of games unassisted. Five tutors are the only redundancy either of them has — and the record shows no substitute exists for the Crawler in the format's colour.
2
EACH OPPONENT, OR TARGET PLAYEROn the identical grinding board, a drain reading 'each opponent' bills every seat at once. Blood Artist, which reads 'target player loses 1 life', removed eight life of a pod's 160. One word of templating, and the Counselor called it not a kill and not close. Sort your drains by that word before you sort them by anything else.
3
THE GROUP WITH NO CARDS IN ITFeed the Swarm is the only entry in the 99 that destroys a noncreature permanent an opponent controls, and it reads creature or enchantment. Artifact: zero cards, by enumeration of all 80 entries' oracle text. Land: zero. The recursion suite wants your own graveyard intact, and the cheapest permanent that seals a graveyard is an artifact.
4
TWO CARDS THE TAXONOMY CANNOT SEERole coverage is 97.5%, and the two it misses are Plumb the Forbidden and Supernatural Stamina — the instant-speed answer to a wrath, and a two-mana trick that both saves the commander and hands you a death trigger. Absence of a role is not absence of the function. Read the card.
Pilot TipNever plan to fund the recursion loops with it — colorless mana does not pay Gravecrawler's {B}. Use it for outlet work and bulk mana.
Thirty-nine combo lines are fully contained in this list, eleven of them two-card infinites. Eight signed files make six of them shapes. Containment is a shortlist, never evidence.

Keep or Ship

by Coach Sunny Brightside with "Ledger" Lin Marginal
★ COACHING◆ DATA-DERIVED
Seven cards, one call. The Coach trusts your gut; Ledger brought receipts.
SEVEN CARDS

SHAPE, NOT NAMES

You keep the first seven 81.6% of the time at a mean of 0.222 mulligans, so the deck is not asking you to gamble. The question is never whether the commander is here — it is whether the hand makes bodies and can spend one for free.

Keepable first sevens (simulated)82%

KEEP

Swamp, Swamp, Swamp, Carrion Feeder, Bitterblossom, Fume Spitter, Zulaport Cutthroat

Three lands is the modal kept hand — 3,561 of 10,000 keeps hold exactly three. This one already carries a free outlet, renewable fodder, an ignition source and an each-opponent drain: the four classes the model finds earliest, at mean draw turns of 2.690, 2.578, 2.818 and 2.582. Nothing in it costs more than two.

KEEP

Swamp, Swamp, Sol Ring, Dark Ritual, Reassembling Skeleton, Vampiric Tutor, Skullclamp

Fast mana is the thinnest class on the sheet — three cards, 43.6% by turn ten — and 718 of 10,000 games cast the commander on turn two, every one off exactly this. The Skeleton gives him something to eat the moment he lands, and a tutor is what carries the fastest kill from 6.6% to 42.6%.

SHIP

Swamp, Swamp, Swamp, Mikaeus, the Unhallowed, Grave Titan, Exquisite Blood, Gray Merchant of Asphodel

All payoff, nothing underneath it. Mean available mana is 3.908 on turn four and 4.606 on turn five, so a six-drop and two five-drops with no enabler in front of them means you do nothing for half the game. Enablers dig toward payoffs. Payoffs sit there waiting for an engine nobody built.

SHIP

Swamp, Gravecrawler, Phyrexian Altar, Bastion of Remembrance, Demonic Tutor, Grave Pact, Necropotence

This is a kill in your hand and it is still a ship. One land — 1,429 of 10,000 first sevens hold exactly one, and one land is a mulligan unless Sol Ring, Ancient Tomb or Dark Ritual sits beside it. The second Zombie the Altar loop needs is not here either. Ship it and you will draw into a shape; keep it and you will watch one.

Most people mulligan this deck hunting for the commander. Don't. He lands on turn four in 7,188 of 10,000 goldfished games with no help at all, so keep the hand that gives him something to eat, or the hand that wins without him.

Keep a shape, not a card list: something that sacrifices, something to sacrifice, and two to four lands. That is what 9,132 of 10,000 kept hands look like, and from there your turn-3 land drop hits 91.6%. The outlet and the fodder are interchangeable, so stop holding out for the good one. Carrion Feeder, Viscera Seer, Woe Strider, Yahenni and Blasting Station all do the outlet job; Bitterblossom, Reassembling Skeleton, Stitcher's Supplier, Ophiomancer and Gravecrawler are every one of them fodder. A free outlet turns up by turn 10 in 78.5% of games — you do not need the specific one in your opener.

Ship the hand that is all payoff, however good it looks. Exquisite Blood, Grave Titan and five lands is a mulligan: mean available mana is 3.908 on turn 4, so a five-drop with nothing under it means you do nothing for half the game. Enablers dig toward payoffs. Payoffs sit there waiting for an engine nobody built.

One land is a ship unless Sol Ring, Ancient Tomb or Dark Ritual is sitting next to it. Zero lands is always a ship, and only 284 opening sevens in 10,000 come out that way, so it will hardly ever be the decision in front of you. Ship freely in general — you keep the first seven 81.6% of the time at a mean of 0.222 mulligans, which makes the first one a trade you are allowed to take on instinct. And take the hand with a tutor over the hand with the commander every time: a tutor is what turns a 6.6% kill into a 42.6% one.

Pilot TipNever ship a hand for lacking him — he arrives on turn four in 7,188 of 10,000 games without any help from you.
Two to five lands, something that sacrifices, something to sacrifice. Only 284 first sevens in 10,000 come out landless — the hard decision almost never arrives.
Keep or ShipMANA MAP · VOL. 008

What's Your Play?

by Coach Sunny Brightside
★ COACHING
Real board, real stakes. Commit before the Coach shows his hand.
COMMIT FIRST

PICK BEFORE YOU READ

One board hands you six mana, a running engine and no kill. The other hands you a one-mana artifact you cannot break. Four branches each — commit out loud before the Coach shows his hand.

Stop asking which payoff is biggest. Ask which one is still on the battlefield next turn.

The engine has paid you for three turns, no kill is in your hand, and six mana is asking what it buys

The board

You

Creatures
Yawgmoth, Thran Physician, Carrion Feeder, Reassembling Skeleton, Faerie Rogue token, Faerie Rogue token
Permanents
Bastion of Remembrance, Bitterblossom
Lands
Swamp, Swamp, Swamp, Swamp, Swamp, Phyrexian Tower, an untapped land
Hand
Grave Pact, Exquisite Blood, Warren Soultrader
Graveyard
Stitcher's Supplier, Fume Spitter, Swamp
Mana available
Six untapped lands producing {B}{B}{B}{B}{B} plus {C}. Grave Pact costs {1}{B}{B}{B}, Exquisite Blood {4}{B}, Warren Soultrader {2}{B} — you can cast exactly one of the three. Your life total is 32.

Player 2 (Golgari midrange) — 29 life

Board
two 3/3 creatures, a 1/1 mana dork, tapped out

Player 3 (Azorius control) — 34 life

Board
no creatures, five untapped lands, held through two rotations, assume a sweeper and one counterspell

Player 4 (Rakdos aggro) — 21 life

Board
a 2/1 attacker, a 3/2 attacker, tapped out after swinging at Player 2
Nobody here is frightened of you, and nothing you are holding kills anybody. The machine is running and the ceiling is missing. So what do the six mana buy — the card that makes you the table's police, the loud half of your best kill, the quiet half of your fastest one, or nothing at all while you dig five cards deep?

Cast Grave Pact and become the table's police

The line
Grave Pact for {1}{B}{B}{B}, leaving {B}{B}. Sacrifice a Faerie to Yawgmoth on the end step before your turn and every other player gives up a creature of their choice. Do it again with the second Faerie. Spend the last {1}{B} returning Reassembling Skeleton and eat that too — three activations, three cards, three rounds of edicts, and both creature boards at this table are gone.
Signals sent
Enormous, and unmistakable. Forced sacrifices are the effect this format resents most, and three rounds of them inside one rotation says your engine converts into other people's permanents on demand.
Coalition risk
The highest on the page, and pointed the wrong way. Every creature you take comes off the two seats that are pressuring the control player, and the control player is the one holding the answer to the plan you actually care about. You would be doing their board management with your own life total.
Read

The card is excellent and this is the wrong turn for it.

Start with what it does not do. It advances no kill. Your problem tonight is not the board — the aggro seat is losing a race it started — your problem is that no configuration is on the table.

Then look at who gets paid. The control seat owns no creatures, so every edict you fire is free value for the one deck that beats you.

And price the attention. Nobody has spent removal on you yet, which is the most valuable thing you own. Three rounds of forced sacrifices is how a pod picks an archenemy, and it spends the deflection budget you will want on the turn you finally go.

Grave Pact is a keeper for the turn an opposing board is genuinely killing you, or the turn it converts a kill you already hold. Play it then and it wins a game. Play it now and you are buying protection you already have.

Cast Exquisite Blood and commit the loud half of your best kill

The line
Exquisite Blood for {4}{B}, leaving {B}. Nothing happens this turn, and nothing happens next turn either unless a body shows up. From there South Wind Avatar turns it into the mandatory loop the checker settled — and Bastion of Remembrance is already on the table supplying the lifegain that lights it.
Signals sent
The most recognisable card in your deck, cast on a turn where it removes nobody's life. Everyone here has seen it before and everyone knows what it is waiting for. The control seat gets a full rotation, and every land it has been holding, to decide what to do about that.
Coalition risk
High, and unusually survivable. Creature removal cannot touch it and only the Golgari seat is likely to hold anything that can. What it really costs is your cover: from here the table stops pricing you as a value deck.
Read

This is your best kill and I am not talking you out of it. I am talking you out of leading with it.

Take the numbers first, because they should govern the rest of your game. The Exquisite Blood shape assembles in 6.9% of goldfished games by turn ten unassisted and 42.5% with a tutor, at a mean turn of 5.301 — the highest rate and the fastest clock of your four kills. Point every search you draw from here at that pair.

Now the part that decides this turn. Five of your six mana buys a permanent that does nothing until a second card arrives, and no tutor has arrived: you are in the 39% of games that never sees one by turn ten.

So the enchantment sits. It sits through a whole rotation with your cover gone, and the only work it does in that time is tell three players what to hold.

One more caution, and it is about your own deck rather than the table. The checker resolved this loop on South Wind Avatar, whose own death trigger supplies the life that starts it. Marauding Blight-Priest and Starscape Cleric carry the identical drain text and gain no life themselves — Bastion covers that, but nobody has resolved it. Treat the Avatar as the sure thing and those two as candidates.

Cast this on the turn the body is already down, or on the turn you can cast both. Not on a turn where it is a five-mana billboard.

Cast Warren Soultrader and keep three mana up

The line
Warren Soultrader for {2}{B}, leaving {B}{B}{B}. Bastion of Remembrance is already down, so Gravecrawler is now the entire rest of the self-funding loop — one named card. Eat a Faerie with Yawgmoth on each opponent's end step for a card apiece, put the counters on the Rakdos attackers, and hold {1}{B} to return Reassembling Skeleton at instant speed if the digging is going well.
Signals sent
A 3/3 for three on a board that already has five bodies. It reads as a fair creature and a chump blocker. Nobody at this table has ever lost to a Warren Soultrader, and nobody is spending an exile effect on one.
Coalition risk
Low, and it stays low. The loud cards stay in your hand, the open mana reads as interaction, and all the table sees is a value deck adding a body. That is a very small price for a permanent that narrows a kill to a single card.
Read

This is the play, and the reason is arithmetic rather than taste.

Count what each option leaves you needing. Grave Pact leaves you needing an entire kill. Exquisite Blood leaves you needing one of three bodies. Soultrader leaves you needing Gravecrawler and nothing else, because the drain half is already on the battlefield.

That is the kill you most want to be one card away from. Each sacrifice makes the Treasure that pays for the next Gravecrawler, each iteration costs a life and hands it straight back, and when you take it every seat reaches zero at the same moment. It is the attempt you can make into open mana and still have a deck if it fails.

Now the part most pilots give away for nothing. Your commander charges no mana at all, so the two Faerie activations are free in every branch on this page — you are not buying digging with these three mana, you already own it. What the three mana buy is an instant-speed Skeleton return, and the moment to spend it is the end step before your own turn, when the table's chances to respond are already gone.

One habit while you do it. Point the counters at the Rakdos attackers, never at your own bodies. Their 2/1 dies to a single activation you were making anyway.

And keep one thing to yourself: Soultrader makes Treasures, so the permanent that reads as a chump blocker is also the engine mana your loops run on.

Cast nothing and dig five cards deep

The line
Spend all six mana on Reassembling Skeleton: feed it to Yawgmoth, return it for {1}{B}, repeat until the mana is gone. Then eat both Faeries. Five activations, five cards, five -1/-1 counters aimed at the Rakdos attackers, and five drains off Bastion of Remembrance — every opponent down five, you back where you started, because Bastion returns each life you pay.
Signals sent
Every life total at the table moves five points in one turn while your board drops from five bodies to two. That is the loudest thing this deck can do without killing anybody, and it reclassifies you from the value player to the drain player in one sitting.
Coalition risk
Moderate, and it arrives late. Nobody dies, but the aggro seat falls to 16 with your blockers eaten, which invites the swing back. And you have shown three players that your engine converts bodies into life loss on demand — the read you have spent seven turns keeping off the table.
Read

The instinct is right and the price is wrong.

Digging is what this deck does. Your commander turns a spare body into a card for no mana, and under Bastion of Remembrance the life you pay comes straight back, so the draw engine is genuinely free. Run it every single turn.

But look at what the six mana actually bought. Two of those five cards were free and the other three cost two mana each. You paid your whole turn for three cards and deployed nothing.

Then count what you fed it. Reassembling Skeleton is renewable fodder and one of the few bodies that comes back forever — recycling it three times in one turn is the most expensive way to buy cards you already own.

And you handed your blockers to an aggro seat with two attackers pointed somewhere.

Take the free half, refuse the expensive half. Eat the two Faeries for two cards, and if nothing on the other side of the table is worth shrinking, choose no target at all — the draw does not depend on one. Then spend the mana on a permanent.

Our call: Cast Warren Soultrader and keep three mana up

Nobody at this table thinks you are winning. Three turns of drawing cards and eating your own tokens reads as a value deck that never got there, and that belief is worth more to you right now than any card in your hand.

So refuse the framing. The question is not which of these three is the strongest card. It is which one leaves you closest to a shape you can finish.

Count it out loud. Grave Pact leaves you needing a whole kill. Exquisite Blood leaves you needing one of three bodies. Warren Soultrader leaves you needing Gravecrawler, and nothing else, because the drain is already on the table.

That is the loop that pays for itself. Each sacrifice buys the Treasure that casts the next Gravecrawler, each iteration costs a life and gives it back, and all three seats hit zero together. It is the one attempt you can make into a live table and still have a deck afterwards.

Then take the digging that was always free. Your commander costs no mana, so eat a Faerie on each opponent's end step for a card apiece and put the counters on the Rakdos attackers while you do it. Two cards, one dead attacker, no mana spent.

Hold the last {1}{B} for a Skeleton return and spend it at the end step before your own turn rather than now. Information first, commitment last.

I will name the risk instead of hiding it. Soultrader is a creature and three seats can kill a creature; Exquisite Blood is an enchantment and one seat can. If the Golgari player spends removal on your 3/3, you have lost a turn and learned exactly where their answer went — and Exquisite Blood is still in your hand.

That is the whole trade. Three mana and a replaceable body to make your best loop one card wide, while looking exactly as harmless as you did an hour ago. Take it, and start digging for free.

A one-mana artifact has switched off three of your four kills and nothing in the 99 destroys an artifact

The board

You

Creatures
Yawgmoth, Thran Physician, Gravecrawler, Reassembling Skeleton, Faerie Rogue token, Faerie Rogue token
Permanents
Bastion of Remembrance, Bitterblossom
Lands
Swamp, Swamp, Swamp, Swamp, Swamp, Phyrexian Tower, an untapped land
Hand
Exquisite Blood, Vampiric Tutor, Feed the Swarm
Graveyard
Stitcher's Supplier, Viscera Seer, Swamp, Swamp
Mana available
Six untapped lands producing {B}{B}{B}{B}{B} plus {C}. Exquisite Blood costs {4}{B}, Vampiric Tutor {B}, Feed the Swarm {1}{B}. Your life total is 34.

Player 2 (Golgari midrange) — 33 life

Board
two 3/3 creatures, a 1/1 mana dork, three untapped lands, assume a second naturalize-style answer; they spent one earlier this game

Player 3 (mono-white hatebears) — 36 life

Board
Grafdigger's Cage, two 2/2 creatures, two untapped lands

Player 4 (Rakdos reanimator) — 25 life

Board
a 2/2 blocker, tapped out, a 7/7 sitting in their graveyard, assume a reanimation spell in hand
You cannot destroy an artifact — no card in the 99 does. Do you spend a real political favour buying an answer, commit your hand to the one kill the Cage cannot touch, hand the problem to the seat it hurts more, or say nothing at all and keep grinding?

Buy the break — pay the Golgari seat to destroy the Cage

The line
Name the Cage out loud and make Player 2 an offer. Their next artifact answer points at it; in exchange you spend Feed the Swarm on whatever is attacking them, you give them first refusal on your commander's counters for two turns, and you leave their board alone while the deal holds. Then rebuild the recursion plan behind it.
Signals sent
The most information you can give away in one sentence. Three players now know which single permanent switches your deck off, and one of them owns it.
Coalition risk
Severe, and it outlives the game. The hatebears seat learns their one-mana artifact is beating you, so they protect it, replace it, or add the second one. You have also taught this pod that the cheapest possible answer to this deck is a cheap artifact, and pods remember.
Read

Before you spend a favour, price what you are buying back.

The Cage takes your three least-likely kills and leaves your most likely one alone. The Soultrader loop assembles in 1.1% of goldfished games unassisted and 18.0% with a tutor; the Phyrexian Altar loop 0.5% and 12.3%; the Mikaeus loop 4.6% and 33.7%. The Exquisite Blood loop is 6.9% and 42.5%, and the Cage does not see it.

So you are being asked to pay a real favour, plus your cover, to unlock the three routes you assemble least.

Be accurate about the damage while you decide, because it is broad. Nothing that returns a creature from your graveyard to the battlefield works — that is your recursion, your reanimation, and every undying body you own, because undying is a return from the graveyard to the battlefield like any other.

Broad is not the same as fatal. Your commander still draws. Bastion of Remembrance still drains. Bitterblossom still makes a body every upkeep. The machine that has paid you all game is untouched, and the kill you are most likely to assemble is untouched with it.

Save the favour. You will want a genuine one later, and a table only lets you overpay once.

Cast Exquisite Blood now and buy the body at the end step

The line
Tap Phyrexian Tower and four Swamps for Exquisite Blood, keeping one Swamp up. On the last opponent's end step, Vampiric Tutor for South Wind Avatar for {B} and 2 life. Draw it, cast it for {3}{B} next turn, then sacrifice a Faerie to Yawgmoth before you pass: Bastion of Remembrance gains you a life, the Avatar drains each opponent, Exquisite Blood gains it straight back, and the loop is mandatory from there — every seat reaches zero at the same moment.
Signals sent
The most recognisable card in your deck, cast into a table that has just watched a one-mana artifact turn you off. Read that as cover rather than exposure — the seat that played the Cage believes it solved you, and this is the one thing it did not solve.
Coalition risk
Real for one turn and for one seat. Creature removal cannot touch an enchantment, so the only player likely to answer it is Player 2, who has already spent one of those this game. Everyone else has to sit and watch it resolve.
Read

This is the play, and the reason is which half can afford to sit.

Three seats at this table can kill a creature. One seat can kill an enchantment. So the enchantment is the piece you put down early, and the body is the piece that never spends a turn on the table at all.

That is why the order matters more than the cards do. Cast the Avatar last and you activate your commander the same turn it lands — his ability costs no mana and you may use it whenever you hold priority, so the pair exists for a few seconds instead of a rotation.

It is also a full turn faster. Buying the body first means casting a 3/4, passing, and asking it to survive three seats before the five-mana half even arrives.

Keep one Swamp up when you tap out. That single black is your tutor, and casting it on the last end step before you untap means their answers are already spent when you take your turn.

And know what you are fetching. The checker resolved this loop on South Wind Avatar specifically, whose own death trigger supplies the life that starts it. Marauding Blight-Priest and Starscape Cleric carry the same drain text and cost less, but nobody has resolved them and this is not the game to find out.

Hide the famous card and put the body down first

The line
Vampiric Tutor for South Wind Avatar on the last opponent's end step for {B} and 2 life, and spend two mana this turn on Feed the Swarm to clear the Golgari mana dork. Draw the Avatar, cast it next turn as a 3/4 deathtouch blocker, and hold Exquisite Blood until a turn you can cast it with your commander untapped and a body to eat.
Signals sent
Almost nothing. A 3/4 deathtouch body on a board full of small ones reads as defence, and it will be the least interesting permanent anybody plays this rotation.
Coalition risk
Low on the surface and mispriced underneath. The Avatar is a creature at a table where three seats hold creature removal, and the card you are carefully hiding is the one three of them cannot touch.
Read

The right instinct, applied to the wrong card.

Hiding the loud half is usually correct in this deck and I would tell you to do it with almost any other pair. It is wrong here because of who holds what.

Creature removal is everywhere at this table. Enchantment removal is one seat, probably once. So the body is the fragile half and the enchantment is the durable one, which is the reverse of how the two cards feel in your hand.

There is a tempo cost stacked on top. This line spends a turn casting a 3/4 and then needs five more mana on the turn after, so you are asking to survive two windows where the other order asks for one.

And the blocker is worth less than it looks. You already have a commander who eats attackers for free by putting counters on them, and you are not short of bodies.

Keep the shape and fix the order. Same two cards, same tutor, one turn sooner and one fewer chance for anybody to act.

Say nothing to the Golgari seat and let the reanimator pay for it

The line
Spend no card and no favour on the Cage. Mention once, to Player 4 and without pressing it, that the creature in their graveyard is not coming back while that artifact is out. Then run the engine: eat both Faeries with Yawgmoth for two cards, put the counters on their blocker, cast Feed the Swarm on a Golgari 3/3, and pass with mana up.
Signals sent
Almost nothing about your deck, and one useful sentence about somebody else's. The table learns the Cage is a problem without learning it is your problem.
Coalition risk
The lowest on the page, and that is exactly why it is tempting. The hatebears seat keeps believing the Cage is aimed at the graveyard deck, which is where they aimed it, and nobody reprices you at all.
Read

This is the cheapest correct sentence available and you should say it in every branch on this page. It simply is not a plan.

The trouble is what the turn does. You spend six mana on a removal spell and two free draws, and you finish exactly as far from a kill as you started.

Waiting also idles the thing you are best at. A tutor takes the Exquisite Blood shape from 6.9% to 42.5%, only 60.6% of games see one by turn ten, and yours is in your hand doing nothing.

And Player 4 may simply not hold an artifact answer. You would be spending a rotation on somebody else's draw step and calling it a plan.

Say the sentence. Then spend the turn on the kill the Cage cannot touch.

Our call: Cast Exquisite Blood now and buy the body at the end step

The hatebears seat believes a one-mana artifact just switched you off, and they are three-quarters right. Let them keep the three quarters and take the one that matters.

Price what the Cage actually costs you. It takes the Soultrader loop at 1.1% unassisted, the Phyrexian Altar loop at 0.5% and the Mikaeus loop at 4.6% — your three least-likely routes. It leaves the Exquisite Blood loop at 6.9%, and 42.5% the moment you spend a tutor, at a mean assembly turn of 5.301. Your most-assembled and fastest kill is the one it does not see.

You are holding half of that kill and a tutor for the other half. That is not a deck that got answered. That is a deck one turn from being the favourite.

So spend the mana on the half that can afford to sit. An enchantment at this table can be answered by one player; a creature can be answered by three. Put Exquisite Blood down now, while your cover is a story about how badly you just got hosed.

Keep one black up when you tap out and buy the Avatar on the last end step before you untap, so their mana is already spent when you draw it.

Then take the whole thing in a single turn. Cast the Avatar, and before you pass, sacrifice a Faerie to your commander — the ability costs nothing and you may use it whenever you hold priority. The loop is mandatory from there and it ends with every seat at zero together, so the table never gets the turn where you are lethal on one player and merely frightening to the rest.

Two riders, both free. Tell the reanimator seat the Cage is eating their graveyard; it costs you nothing and might buy the answer you refused to pay for. And keep Feed the Swarm for the creature or enchantment that genuinely threatens you, because it was never going to touch that artifact.

Do not buy the break. A favour is the most expensive currency you own, and you would be spending it to unlock the three kills you assemble least while telling three players precisely how to beat you. You already have the one they cannot switch off. Go and get the second half.

What's Your Play?MANA MAP · VOL. 008

The Kill

by Counselor Vera Dictum
✓ RULES-VERIFIED
The winning lines, argued and affirmed. Every step on the record.
ON THE RECORD

SIX SHAPES, SIX BOUNDS

Eleven case files, six kills. Two run forever, one wins because it cannot stop, two you count against your own library, and the one you will play most is a pace. Every step on the record.

1
THE PRIMARY IS A PACENest of Scarabs plus any of nine drains, Yawgmoth working from the zone — files 002, 003 and 004. One rotation is bounded by its own bodies at five to eight activations, roughly two drains per body, and protection from Humans is why it stops: he cannot target himself, so once the opponent's creature is gone every legal target is one of your own tokens. The kill is rotations stacked across turns — the table's life falls in installments, and by turn six the pieces are together in 7.5% of games on your own draws, 38.9% with a wish.
2
UNBOUNDED: IT FUNDS ITSELFWarren Soultrader, Gravecrawler, Bastion of Remembrance — file 009. Gravecrawler's ability modifies the zone its own card may be cast from, so the permission is live in the graveyard every iteration, the Zombie condition held by Soultrader — whom 'another creature' keeps out of the loop forever. Each activation's Treasure exactly funds the next cast. Your life nets zero. Each opponent pays one per lap, for as long as you care to lap.
3
UNBOUNDED: IT PAYS YOUSwap the Treasure for Phyrexian Altar — file 011. The Altar's ability needs no target and could add mana, so it is a mana ability and never uses the stack: the {B} is in your pool before anyone receives priority, and there is nothing to respond to. Carrion Feeder holds the Zombie condition and is never the sacrifice. Each lap gains you a life, the board returns to an identical state, and you finish the game at 80.
4
MANDATORY, AND NOT A DRAWExquisite Blood beside a body reading 'Whenever you gain life, each opponent loses 1 life' — file 012. Neither card says 'may', so the loop is compulsory, and the Counselor's favourite question is whether that forces a draw. It does not: a draw needs a sequence that could be repeated indefinitely, and every cycle here lowers every opponent's life, so there is a way to stop. Drain 40 takes all opponents from 1 to 0 at one state-based check, and they leave together. You are still there, so you win.
5
COUNTED: 39 AGAINST 59Mikaeus grants undying, and your commander's -1/-1 counter does not shrink the returned body — it annihilates the +1/+1 undying left behind and re-arms the creature to die again. File 010. But the commander's draw has no 'may', so your library is the clock: 39 further activations needed against the 59 it permits, a margin of 20. One trap the record is explicit about: Viscera Seer's sacrifice places no counter, so a mid-loop activation through her costs a body that cannot be re-armed.
6
COUNTED: 30 AGAINST 55Butcher Ghoul and Geralf's Messenger alternating under Yawgmoth, a drain down — file 014. The alternation is genuine: each undying return leaves a +1/+1, the commander's counter strips it by state-based action, and the body dies counter-free again — 'unbounded' is refuted only because every activation is a mandatory draw against a library of roughly 55. The bound never saves the table: each two-activation cycle takes 2 from every opponent and 4 from the Messenger's seat at net-zero life to you, so about 30 activations end the pod with roughly 25 draws of margin. Count before you announce, never during.
7
ONE WORD, AND THE POD SURVIVESBlood Artist reads 'target player loses 1 life' where the closers read each opponent — file 004. On the identical grinding board: eight triggers, eight life removed of a pod's 160, the arithmetic divided by four with an exhaustive search finding nothing better. Refuted as a kill, and not narrowly. Play him as reach and wrath insurance, never as the finish.
8
THREE EDGES FOR THE ROADFile 005: an Insect killed by its own counter dies at toughness 0, and gaining zero is not gaining — a lifegain-metered drain sees nothing. File 006: a creature that enters already wearing a -1/-1 counter does trigger Nest of Scarabs, because a reference to putting counters covers counters it enters with. File 008: a cost is paid while the ability is already on the stack, so one activation paid with a token pays a create-or-sacrifice drain twice.
Pilot TipKeep her out of the undying loops mid-stream — her sacrifice places no counter, and only your commander's counter re-arms a returned body. Save her for the finish.
An unbounded loop is a decision. A counted loop is arithmetic. The engine is a pace. Know which one you are announcing before you go.
Verified line 002

Yawgmoth + Nest of Scarabs + Bastion of Remembrance: what bounds the engine?

The clause is “up to one target creature”, and targets are locked in when the ability is activated (CR 115.1c) — the Insect the Nest is about to make does not yet exist to be chosen. Protection from Humans keeps Yawgmoth off his own list (CR 702.16b). So the moment the opponent's creature is dead, every legal target is one of your own tokens: two bodies leave, one comes back. The holding: this engine draws cards and then stops. It is not a kill, and no ordering makes it one.

  1. Yawgmoth, Thran Physician's activated ability — 'Pay 1 life, Sacrifice another creature: Put a -1/-1 counter on up to one target creature and draw a card.' Cost already paid: 1 life paid (you are at 39) and Insect token A sacrificed. Target chosen: Grizzly Bears.
    controlled by you

Result. NOT unbounded. Reading the board as the scenario annotates it - Insect token A is already sacrificed to pay the cost of the ability on the stack, so you have THREE sacrificeable bodies counting it (A, Insect B, Human Soldier token) - the engine is bounded by bodies. Once Grizzly Bears is dead, every legal target for Yawgmoth's first ability is one of your own 1/1 tokens (Yawgmoth cannot target himself through protection from Humans; the two enchantments are not creatures; the Insect that Nest of Scarabs will make does not exist when the target is chosen), so each iteration costs two bodies and returns one - net minus one body per activation. From this board there are exactly FIVE completed activations of the sacrifice ability, ended by having no other creature to sacrifice. The opponent's 2-toughness creature does not bound the engine, it EXTENDS it by two activations; your life goes UP, and your library is untouched at 55. THE BOARD'S NUMBER IS SEVEN: seven creatures you controlled die (five sacrificed, two counter-killed), Bastion of Remembrance triggers seven times, your life 39 -> 42, each opponent 40 -> 33, 28 total life removed from a pod of 160. Net life per activation for you is 0 while the counter goes onto the opponent's creature or nowhere, and +1 on the two true engine iterations. No opponent comes near losing: the lowest life total on the table is 33, so the life-total state-based action never fires (step 11). A LINE that also spends {B}{B} and a card on Yawgmoth's proliferate reaches 8 drains / 32 life removed / you at 43 without changing the five-activation count (step 10) - that is a property of the line, not of the board, and this final_state describes the board invariant.

Verified line 003

Yawgmoth + Nest of Scarabs + Zulaport Cutthroat: does a creature drain survive its own engine?

Three permissions, and not a restriction among them. You choose which creature pays “Sacrifice another creature” (CR 601.2h), “up to one target” permits choosing none (CR 115.6), and a leaves-the-battlefield trigger looks back at the board as it was (CR 603.10a). Nothing here can take Zulaport Cutthroat away from you against your will. He survives every iteration and is spent as the deliberate final sacrifice, where his own trigger banks the seventh drain. The holding: seven drains, and the table lives through them.

On the stack, top first
  1. Yawgmoth, Thran Physician's activated ability — 'Pay 1 life, Sacrifice another creature: Put a -1/-1 counter on up to one target creature and draw a card.' Cost already paid: 1 life paid (you are at 39) and Insect token A sacrificed. Target chosen: Grizzly Bears.
    controlled by you
    — resolves first

Result. Reading taken (stated, not assumed): the scenario's board annotates Insect token A as already sacrificed to pay the cost of the ability on the stack, so the pilot enters this resolution with ONE live insect (token B). The pilot is NEVER forced to consume Zulaport Cutthroat: Yawgmoth's -1/-1 ability is a cost-paid activation (113.3b), the pilot chooses which creature pays 'Sacrifice another creature' (601.2h), and 'up to one target creature' permits zero targets (115.6) — so neither the cost nor the effect can reach Zulaport against the pilot's will. Zulaport survives every iteration and is spent only as the deliberate final sacrifice, where its own leaves-the-battlefield trigger looks back in time (603.6c, 603.10a) and banks the seventh drain. Six of the seven drains are TOKEN deaths, and they count because 704.5f puts the 0/0 token into its owner's graveyard and 704.5d removes it only on the following state-based-action check (117.5) — the token dies first and ceases to exist second, which is what makes each one a 'dies' event (700.4). The engine is nonetheless strictly finite: each activation consumes one body and Nest of Scarabs returns at most one, so the loop is body-neutral only while a creature can take a -1/-1 counter and live. Grizzly Bears (2/2) is the only such sink and absorbs exactly two counters; Yawgmoth is 2/4 but his protection from Humans makes him untargetable by his own ability (702.16b, 113.7). Optimal line: resolve the stacked activation, then spend {B}{B} + a discard on Yawgmoth's proliferate to put the second counter on Bears — that buys a free Insect token without spending a sacrifice, and is worth exactly one extra drain over using an activation's counter on Bears (7 vs 6). Result: FIVE completed activations of the -1/-1 ability plus one proliferate; SEVEN deaths of creatures you control; each opponent loses 7 life; 28 total life across the table. 160 opposing life becomes 132. NOT LETHAL.

Verified line 004

Yawgmoth + Nest of Scarabs + Blood Artist: can a single-target drain close a four-player table?

Four words decide the case. Blood Artist reads “target player loses 1 life” — one seat per trigger, chosen as the trigger goes on the stack — where the drains that end games read each opponent. The engine also finishes by eating its own payoff: protection bars the commander as a target and “another creature” bars him as the sacrifice, so the Artist is the last legal thing left to point at. The holding: eight triggers, eight life, against a pod holding 160. The arithmetic divides by four and never recovers.

The board

You

Yawgmoth, Thran Physician, Blood Artist, Insect token B
Permanents

On the stack, top first
  1. Yawgmoth, Thran Physician's activated ability — 'Pay 1 life, Sacrifice another creature: Put a -1/-1 counter on up to one target creature and draw a card.' Cost already paid: 1 life paid (you are at 39) and Insect token A sacrificed. Target chosen: Grizzly Bears.
    controlled by you
    — resolves first

Result. NOT A KILL, AND NOT EVEN CLOSE — a further refutation of the 'near-infinite' family on this deck, for a different reason than stack 001. This file claims NO ordinal: sister stack 003 holds the 'second time' on this deck and this refutation comes after it. The Yawgmoth + Nest of Scarabs engine is body-neutral only while the creature taking the -1/-1 counter either survives it or is not yours. On this board exactly one creature survives a counter: Grizzly Bears, toughness 2, and its single survivable counter is spent by the ability already on the stack. After that every legal target is a 1/1 Insect or the 0/1 Blood Artist, and killing one of your own creatures makes the activation minus one body by plain arithmetic — two of your creatures leave, Nest returns one. (The replacement token also arrives after the death, since state-based actions run before waiting triggers reach the stack; that is why it can never be the same activation's target, but it is not what makes the engine body-negative.) Yawgmoth's protection from Humans bars him as a target and 'another creature' bars him as the sacrifice, so the last legal target is Blood Artist itself and the engine ends by eating its own payoff. THE BOARD'S NUMBER IS EIGHT: eight creature deaths with Blood Artist on the battlefield, eight triggers, eight life removed, and exhaustive line-space search finds nothing better. The ACTIVATION COUNT is line-dependent and is not a property of the board: the line resolved here runs four draining sacrifice activations plus one proliferate (and a fifth, non-draining sacrifice activation is then available), while an equally optimal zero-target line runs FIVE draining sacrifice activations for the same eight drains, ending at 43 life with 55 cards and no Insect token G. The proliferate is worth exactly one extra drain because its cost is mana and a card rather than a creature: it removes the Bears while spending no body, and on this board remaining reach is (drains so far) + 2 x (your Insects). Without it the figure is seven. The engine is bounded by bodies and legal targets — not by your library (56 of 60 remain in the line shown), not by your life (40 -> 44, a net gain), and not by any mandatory loop, because the chain terminates on its own. Total reach: 8 life out of 160, spread 2 / 2 / 2 / 2 or concentrated for 40 -> 32 on one player. The answer to (b) is NO, short by 152. The single-target penalty is two different factors, and the split is about BODIES, not about Grizzly Bears: both substitutes read 'creature you control' and both miss the Bears identically. This board holds three creature bodies (Blood Artist, Insect A, Insect B). Zulaport Cutthroat IS a creature, so swapping it in keeps three bodies, it dies as the engine's final act and sees its own death — seven own-creature deaths, 7 x 4 = 28 life, a 3.5x penalty, and the same 28 sister stack 003 reaches on a board of this shape. Bastion of Remembrance is an ENCHANTMENT and never dies, so swapping it in removes a body: the terminal activation has no legal creature target, takes zero targets, and only six own creatures die — 6 x 4 = 24 life, a 3.0x penalty. Both substitution figures are WITH-proliferate and therefore compare only against with-proliferate figures. 003's Zulaport 28 is like-for-like — identical board but for the drainer slot, proliferate also taken — and agrees exactly. Sister stack 002's headline 28 is its NO-proliferate BOARD INVARIANT; 002's comparable figure is its own proliferate_variant of 8 deaths / 32 life / opponents at 32, and 002 states the two are never blended — so the like-for-like Bastion comparison is 24 against 32, not against 28. That 8-life gap is exactly ONE BODY: 002's board carries a 1/1 white Human Soldier token (the token Bastion's own ETB makes) that this board does not, so 002 runs three creature bodies to this board's two, and by step 9's own rule a body is worth +2 deaths / +8 life — 6 deaths / 24 here becomes 8 deaths / 32 there. It is a two-death delta, not a one-death 'restored seventh death'. Neither figure corrects 002. Deckbuilding consequence: Blood Artist is a reach card only against ONE player, and the Yawgmoth engine needs a high-toughness creature (or a recursive body) to be more than a five-death burst — no amount of table presence fixes a drain that divides by four.

Verified line 005

Yawgmoth + Nest of Scarabs + South Wind Avatar: a lifegain-triggered drain, and a 4-toughness body

CR 119.9 is the clause, and it is a small one: an event that gains you 0 life is not a life-gain event at all. An Insect killed by its own -1/-1 counter dies at toughness 0, so South Wind Avatar's dies-trigger resolves for nothing and the second ability never fires. The holding: eight dies-triggers, seven drains. The Avatar's 4 toughness is what does the real work here, absorbing counters that a 1/1 drainer could not take.

South Wind Avatar's dies-trigger fired 8 times but produced only 7 nonzero lifegain events: a sacrificed counterless Insect dies at last known toughness 1 (gain 1), while an Insect killed by its own -1/-1 counter dies at toughness 0 (gain 0, and per CR 119.9 that is not a lifegain event, so no drain). The drain therefore fired 7 times for 1 life per opponent: 28 total opposing life, each opponent 40 -> 33. The Avatar's 4 toughness extends the bound by exactly +4 activations (4 of 8) and supplies all of the damage. The ceiling is proved by case exhaustion over how the Avatar leaves the battlefield (step 11), and Yawgmoth's second ability, '{B}{B}, Discard a card: Proliferate', is explicitly ruled out as a way to beat it (step 10). Spellbook's 'Near-infinite' tag is wrong for this board.

Verified line 006

Persist + Nest of Scarabs: does a creature that ENTERS with a -1/-1 counter trigger the Nest?

CR 122.6 is broader than it first reads: a reference to putting counters on an object covers an object given counters as it enters, not only counters placed on something already sitting there. Persist names no player, so 122.6a makes you the one placing it — which is precisely what Nest of Scarabs asks for. The holding: the Nest triggers and you get an Insect, even though the returned Fume Spitter is back in the graveyard before that trigger reaches the stack. It had already triggered.

The board

You

Permanents
Nest of Scarabs
Lands
Swamp, Swamp, Swamp
Graveyard
Fume Spitter (creature card, nonlegendary)
Mana available
{B}{B}{B} from three untapped Swamps (Persist's cost is already paid)

Opponent A — 40 life

Board
no permanents

Opponent B — 40 life

Board
no permanents

Opponent C — 40 life

Board
no permanents

Opponent D — 40 life

Board
no permanents
On the stack, top first
  1. Persist — 'Return target nonlegendary creature card from your graveyard to the battlefield with a -1/-1 counter on it.' Cost already paid ({1}{B}). Target chosen: Fume Spitter in your graveyard.
    controlled by you
    — resolves first

Result. YES — Nest of Scarabs triggers, and rule 122.6 is what decides it: a reference to counters being put on an object covers an object that is GIVEN counters as it enters the battlefield, not only counters placed on something already there. Persist names no player, so 122.6a assigns the counter to Fume Spitter's controller, which is you — satisfying 'whenever you put one or more -1/-1 counters on a creature.' You get one 1/1 black Insect token. The counter also kills the creature it rode in on: Fume Spitter is a 1/1, enters as a 0/0, and is put into the graveyard by state-based actions before the trigger even reaches the stack — the trigger is unaffected because it had already triggered. The answer does NOT differ for a counter placed on a creature already on the battlefield: that case is YES too, via 608.2c naming you as the actor instead of 122.6a. The single divergence between the two cases is controller-dependent — a creature entering under an opponent's control with a -1/-1 counter has that counter put on by THAT player, so 'whenever you put' would not trigger; that does not apply here.

Verified line 008

Mirkwood Bats + Yawgmoth + Nest of Scarabs: does one activation pay the drain twice?

Savour the order of operations. A cost is paid after the ability is already the topmost object on the stack (CR 602.2a into 601.2h), so the trigger the sacrifice causes lands above it and resolves first. One activation paid with a token therefore pays Mirkwood Bats twice: once for the sacrifice, once for the creation two resolutions later. One caveat for the record — a token killed by its own counter is put into the graveyard by the game rather than sacrificed by you (CR 704.5f), and pays the Bats on creation only.

The board

You

Creatures
Yawgmoth, Thran Physician, Mirkwood Bats, Insect token B
Permanents
Nest of Scarabs
Lands
Swamp, Swamp, Swamp
Already paid
Insect token A

Opponent A — 40 life

Grizzly Bears (2/2, untapped, no counters)

no permanents
