no permanents
On the stack, top first
  1. Yawgmoth, Thran Physician's activated ability — 'Pay 1 life, Sacrifice another creature: Put a -1/-1 counter on up to one target creature and draw a card.' Cost already paid: 1 life paid (you are at 39) and Insect token A sacrificed. Target chosen: Grizzly Bears.
    controlled by you
    — resolves first

Result. ANSWER (a): ONE Yawgmoth activation paid with an Insect token produces TWO Mirkwood Bats triggers, and they never sit on the stack together. Bats reads 'Whenever you create or sacrifice a token' and a single activation supplies one of each: the sacrifice is an ACTIVATION COST, paid after the ability is already the topmost object on the stack (602.2a, 602.2b -> 601.2h), so the sacrifice trigger is put on the stack ABOVE the ability (117.2a, 603.3) and resolves BEFORE it; the creation does not happen until Nest of Scarabs' own trigger resolves two resolutions later and puts a token onto the battlefield (701.7a). An activation that places no counter is worth ONE (sacrifice half only). Bats says 'a token', singular, and 603.2c settles the plural case both ways: once per trigger event, but repeatedly if one event contains multiple occurrences -- so a Nest trigger that placed N counters at once would create N tokens in one event and trigger Bats N times, though every Nest trigger in this line places exactly one counter and is worth exactly one. Counter-KILLED Insects pay nothing on the sacrifice half: 704.5f is the game putting a 0/0 into the graveyard, not the controller sacrificing it (701.21a), so those bodies pay Bats only once, when they were created. ANSWER (b): THIRTEEN Bats triggers -- six sacrifices (Insects A, B, C, E, G, I) and seven creations (C, D, E, F from the sink phase including two from a single proliferate, then G, H, I). Each opponent loses 13 life, 40 -> 27; 52 life removed from the table; 160 opposing life -> 108. MORE THAN BOTH SIBLING ENGINES -- BUT THEY MUST BE QUOTED SEPARATELY, BECAUSE ONLY 003'S BOARD IS THIS BOARD. Like-for-like against stack 003: 003 is the same three-body board with Zulaport Cutthroat in the drainer slot, and its checker-passed 7 drains / 28 opposing life is a WITH-proliferate figure, exactly as this line is -- 13 drains vs 7, 52 pod life vs 28, +24 life, 86% more. Stack 002 is NOT this board and its 28 is not comparable to this line: 002's board carries a fourth body, the 1/1 white Human Soldier token Bastion of Remembrance's own enters-the-battlefield ability creates (an enchantment cannot be a body), and its headline 7 drains / 28 life is its explicit NO-proliferate board invariant while this line spends the proliferate. 002's comparable with-proliferate figure is 8 drains / 32 opposing life; against that, 13 drains vs 8 and 52 pod life vs 32, +20 life, +62.5%. Never present 002, 003 and this stack as one result on one board. Two structural reasons, and the second is the one that would be missed: a creation-and-sacrifice drainer is paid TWICE per activation where a death drainer is paid once per body; and Mirkwood Bats' 2/3 body absorbs two -1/-1 counters and lives at 0/1 (122.1a, 704.5f), which a 1/1 drainer cannot do -- Bats' first counter slot buys one body-neutral activation, and its second slot makes the single {B}{B} proliferate worth a second Nest token, which funds the other activation. Only the first of the two is an activation's counter event, and it alone is what qualifies Bats as proliferate's second counter-bearing permanent (Bears and Bats both carrying a counter) instead of Bears alone; the second counter is placed BY the proliferate, which can only grow a permanent that already has one, so it was never a counter one of your Insects could have taken instead. The bound is still strictly FINITE, not infinite: Yawgmoth cannot target himself (protection from Humans, 702.16b), Bears absorbs two counters and Bats two, and after that every activation is net -1 body. The cost is your own life: Bats gains you nothing, so you end at 34 where 003's Zulaport Cutthroat -- which gains you 1 life per drain -- left you at 42 (002 ends at 42 on its no-proliferate invariant and 43 on its proliferate line). NOT LETHAL -- 52 of 160 opposing life.

Verified line 009

Warren Soultrader + Gravecrawler + Bastion of Remembrance: is the drain unbounded, and what does one iteration cost?

The elegant clause is CR 113.6f: an ability that modifies which zones its own card may be cast from functions in that zone. Gravecrawler's permission is therefore live in the graveyard every iteration, its Zombie condition met by Warren Soultrader, whom “another creature” keeps permanently out of the loop. Each Treasure is an artifact and not a creature, so it funds the recast without restriction. The holding: the loop pays for itself, one drain a lap, and nothing on this board brings it to an end.

The board

On the stack, top first
  1. Bastion of Remembrance's triggered ability — 'Whenever a creature you control dies, each opponent loses 1 life and you gain 1 life.' Triggered on Gravecrawler's death during cost payment.
    controlled by you
    — resolves first
  2. Warren Soultrader's activated ability — 'Pay 1 life, Sacrifice another creature: Create a Treasure token.' Both costs already paid: 1 life paid, Gravecrawler sacrificed.
    controlled by you

Result. The stack as given resolves top-down: Bastion of Remembrance's trigger first (each opponent 40 → 39, you 39 → 40), then Warren Soultrader's ability, creating one Treasure. One full further iteration — cast Gravecrawler from the graveyard for {B} paid by sacrificing that Treasure, then pay 1 life and sacrifice Gravecrawler to Soultrader — leaves each opponent at 38 and returns the board to an identical state. Both halves of part (1) are YES: Gravecrawler's own ability modifies which zone it can be cast from and so functions in the graveyard (113.6f), granting permission on every iteration with its Zombie condition met by Warren Soultrader, which the loop never sacrifices ('another creature'); and each activation's Treasure exactly funds the next {B}, its ability meeting the definition of a mana ability (605.1a) and being unrestricted because a Treasure is an artifact, not a creature. Exactly one Bastion trigger occurs per iteration: only Gravecrawler's death matches its trigger event, so neither creating nor sacrificing a Treasure adds drain (603.2). Part (2): per completed iteration your life is net 0 (dipping to 39 and refunded), each opponent is −1, and Treasures are net 0 (steady state 1). The loop is self-funding and unbounded; 40 drain events bring a seat to exactly 0 — one has already resolved, so 39 further iterations take all four opponents to 0 at once. The Commander Spellbook tag 'Infinite lifeloss' is confirmed for this exact board.

Full dossier: Judge's Desk, Case A-009 → ✓ RULES-VERIFIED cleared in ? review cycle(s)
Verified line 010

Mikaeus + Yawgmoth: does the -1/-1 counter annihilate undying's +1/+1 and re-arm the body, and what bounds the loop?

CR 704.5q is the crux — a permanent holding both a +1/+1 and a -1/-1 counter loses one of each to state-based actions. So the commander's counter does not shrink an undying body. It strips the +1/+1 that undying left behind and re-arms the creature to die again. The holding: the alternation is real, and the loop is finite, because the draw is mandatory. It needs 39 activations against the 59 the library permits, a margin of 20. One trap the record is explicit about: Viscera Seer's own sacrifice places no counter, and only the commander's counter can strip an undying return, so a mid-loop activation costs a body that cannot be re-armed.

  1. Undying triggered ability granted to Viscera Seer by Mikaeus, the Unhallowed — 'When a creature with undying dies, if it had no +1/+1 counters on it, return it to the battlefield under its owner's control with a +1/+1 counter on it.' Triggered on Viscera Seer's death during cost payment.
    controlled by you

  2. Bastion of Remembrance's triggered ability — 'Whenever a creature you control dies, each opponent loses 1 life and you gain 1 life.' Triggered on Viscera Seer's death during cost payment.
    controlled by you
  3. Yawgmoth, Thran Physician's activated ability — 'Pay 1 life, Sacrifice another creature: Put a -1/-1 counter on up to one target creature and draw a card.' Both costs already paid: 1 life paid, Viscera Seer sacrificed. Target chosen: Gravecrawler.
    controlled by you

Result. The stack fully resolves. Undying returns Viscera Seer (its condition read off the creature as it last existed on the battlefield, where it had no counters) with a +1/+1 counter, so it is 3/3. Bastion drains 1 life PER OPPONENT (4 seats x 1 = 4 across the pod, 160 -> 156) and gains you 1, putting you back to 40. Yawgmoth's ability then resolves on its still-legal target: Gravecrawler takes a -1/-1 counter and you draw. THE CRUX: the next state-based-action check removes one +1/+1 and one -1/-1 counter from Gravecrawler simultaneously (704.5q), leaving it with ZERO counters at 3/2 and alive — so YES, Gravecrawler is eligible for undying the next time it dies. The -1/-1 counter is not a shrink, it is a RESET. The same check is verified on the other body: Viscera Seer, printed 1/1, is 2/2 when it holds both counters, so toughness 2 clears 704.5f and it survives to be re-armed in its turn — the alternation holds in both directions. Repetition is legal: priority returns to you with the stack empty and a player may activate an activated ability any time they have priority (117.1b), and the ability needs no mana. The loop is FINITE: life is flat (pay 1, Bastion returns 1), the drain clock needs 39 more activations to zero all four opponents, and the draw is mandatory (608.2c — no 'may' on the card), capping the engine at 59 more activations before you deck yourself. THE TRAP, corrected from the previous pass: Viscera Seer's own free 'Sacrifice a creature: Scry 1' outlet is NOT a strictly favourable bonus. It places no counter, and only Yawgmoth's -1/-1 counter can strip the +1/+1 counter an undying return leaves behind, one per activation — so a mid-loop Seer sacrifice either loads BOTH bodies (sac the armed one; 702.93a returns it with a counter) or permanently loses one (sac the loaded one; undying's intervening 'if' fails). The two branches differ in WHERE the permanent loss falls, and the previous pass's 'either way' sentence was wrong about it: in branch A (armed body eaten) nothing is lost yet and the NEXT Yawgmoth sacrifice is the one that loses a body for good; in branch B (loaded body eaten) the loss has ALREADY happened and the survivor is armed, so the next Yawgmoth sacrifice loses nothing permanently. The conclusion is unchanged because both branches converge on ONE alternation body, and one body can never be re-armed: an activation's single -1/-1 counter can never land on the body that same activation sacrificed to pay its cost — that body is no longer in the zone it was in when it was targeted, so it is an illegal target (608.2b) — and re-arming therefore always costs a different body's death, which with one body left means eating Mikaeus (who is the source of undying) or nothing. Fire the Seer mid-loop and you trade 1 drain per seat for roughly 34 of the 39 activations you need. Use it ONLY as a finisher, where at most FIVE such sacrifices exist — CORRECTED from three: the Seer's cost is 'Sacrifice a creature', NOT 'another creature', so it also eats Mikaeus (whose 'Other non-Human' grant excludes himself) and Yawgmoth (a Human Cleric, whom that grant never reached), and finally itself; four bodies plus the one available undying return is 5, and six is impossible. Margin is therefore 20 activations clean and at most 25 with all five finisher sacrifices — a bounded range, not an open lower bound. All four opponents reach 0 together and leave the game; you are still in it, so you win (104.2a). VERDICT on the deck's declared second kill: the Mikaeus + Yawgmoth line is REAL and lethal on this board, but it is a library-bounded loop, not an infinite combo. A containment record is not a verification; this resolves one of the nineteen and it holds with 20 activations of margin (20-25 depending on finisher Seer sacrifices).

Gravecrawler + Phyrexian Altar + Carrion Feeder + Bastion of Remembrance: is the drain unbounded and does it cost anything?

One line of rules text carries the whole thing. Phyrexian Altar's ability needs no target and could add mana, so it is a mana ability (CR 605.1a) and never uses the stack (CR 605.3b) — the {B} is in the pool before anyone receives priority, and there is nothing to respond to. Sacrificing Gravecrawler refunds exactly the mana that recasts him, with Carrion Feeder holding the Zombie condition and never being the sacrifice. The holding: unbounded, and free — each lap costs nothing and gains you a life.

Verified line 012

Exquisite Blood + South Wind Avatar: the loop is mandatory — does it kill the pod or draw the game?

Counsel's favourite kind of question: the loop is mandatory — neither card says “may” — so does the game simply end in a draw? No, and CR 732.1b is why. A draw requires a sequence that could be repeated indefinitely, and this one cannot, because every cycle takes a life from every opponent. There is a way to stop, which is all CR 104.4b asks. The holding: drain 40 takes all four opponents to 0, state-based actions are checked before the resulting triggers reach the stack, and they leave together (CR 704.5a). You are still there, so you win (CR 104.2a).

The undying pair: Butcher Ghoul + Geralf's Messenger under Yawgmoth — is the loop unbounded, and what does each opponent lose per cycle?

The clause that decides this case is one the deck usually loves: "and draw a card" — no "may" anywhere in it, and a resolving ability's instructions must be followed (CR 608.2c). The alternation itself is genuine and elegant: each undying return leaves a +1/+1 counter, the commander's -1/-1 annihilates it by state-based action (CR 704.5q), and the body dies counter-free again, forever in principle. But "unbounded" is refuted on the record, because every activation is a mandatory draw against a library of roughly 55, and the draw after the last one loses the game (CR 121.4, 704.5b). The holding: library-bounded at ~55 activations — and the bound never saves the table, because each two-activation cycle takes 2 from every opponent and 4 from the Messenger's target at net-zero life to the pilot, so ~30 activations end the pod with ~25 draws of margin.

The KillMANA MAP · VOL. 008

Table Manners

by Coach Sunny Brightside
★ COACHING
Three opponents, one you. How to win friends and eliminate people.
THREE OPPONENTS

NOTHING, THEN EVERYTHING

There is no board where you are lethal on one seat and merely scary to the rest — every signed shape ends the table together. So what do three opponents actually read off your permanents, and how do you spend the misread?

The table has a plan for you, and they made it before you sat down: kill the doctor, the problem goes away. Let them keep it. It is half right, and the wrong half is where your games come from.

Three of your six verified kills never need Yawgmoth on the battlefield — the Soultrader loop (stack 009), the Altar loop (stack 011), Exquisite Blood (stack 012). Three gate on him, including the primary Nest engine (stacks 002 through 004) and both library-bounded lines (stacks 010 and 014). So the rule is phase, not stance. In the grind, trade him cheerfully — he recasts from the command zone and the tax is two mana and a turn. On the engine turn, protect him absolutely.

Your protection is exactly three cards: Lightning Greaves, Supernatural Stamina, Malakir Rebirth. They are engine-turn insurance, nothing else. Never spend one saving a value creature in the mid-game; the turn they exist for is the one where a commander-gated kill is live and the table has mana up.

He lands turn four in 7,188 of 10,000 goldfished games and turn two in 718. Fast mana is three cards — Sol Ring, Ancient Tomb, Dark Ritual — in hand by turn ten in 43.6% of games, so the turn-2 doctor is a windfall, not a plan. Keep the hand that gives him bodies to eat, not the hand that promises him early.

Now the game you will actually play most nights. By turn ten you hold a drain 82.3% of the time, renewable fodder 82.3%, a free outlet 78.5%, an ignition source 73.8% — and your best kill assembles unassisted in 13.5% of games. Read that gap as a job description. Run the value engine, look like the value seat, and dig.

Digging is what he is for. His ability costs no mana and the draw is stapled on, so sacrifice at instant speed on other people's turns and bank cards. Ophiomancer's upkeep Snake and Pawn of Ulamog's Spawn pay him in his own currency without costing you a card — feed him those and keep your hand for pieces.

One feeding rule: Gravecrawler goes in the machine only while a second Zombie is on the battlefield, because the graveyard recast needs a Zombie staring back. With that condition met, sacrifice it freely — it returns for one black, and that permission is the entire reason both unbounded kills exist. As your only Zombie, it is a kill half. Protect it like one.

Tutors: five of them, drawn by turn ten in 61.2% of games, and they take the primary line from 7.5% to 38.9% assembled by turn six — the biggest number swing this deck owns. So a tutor is a kill half, and Nest of Scarabs is the default fetch. A tutor cast for value is the pop-off you didn't have two turns later.

The primary is a pace, not a moment. Nest of Scarabs plus any of nine drains, Yawgmoth working from the zone: one rotation exhausts its own bodies at five to eight activations, roughly two drains per body (stacks 002 and 005), so the table's life falls in installments across turns. Point the counters at their creatures whenever a legal target exists — stacks 002 and 003 measured an opposing two-toughness body extending the rotation by two activations. Free removal and extra fuel, same habit.

Count the library before you go off. The doctor's draw has no "may" in it, so both undying lines end when your library does. The Mikaeus line needs 39 more activations against the 59 your library permits (stack 010). The undying pair — Butcher Ghoul plus Geralf's Messenger — has about 55 activations available and is lethal near 30: each two-activation cycle takes 2 from every opponent and 4 from the seat the Messenger hits, 8 pod life per cycle, at zero net life to you (stack 014). That is roughly twenty-five draws of margin, which is plenty — if you counted first. Announce the loop with the library number in hand; an uncounted loop that eats removal halfway leaves you hellbent over ten cards with no kill in them.

When does the table flip on you? Not when the board gets wide — when a second noncreature payoff resolves. Exquisite Blood. Phyrexian Altar beside a Gravecrawler. Mikaeus untapping. So sequence the loud half last: cheap halves early under the value costume, Nykthos held to convert all those black pips into the engine turn's mana, and the reveal at the end step before your own turn. Count what is open first — "assume every held answer appears the moment you present lethal, and count the table's open mana before committing". Your attempts are cheap by that school's own standard: stack 009 funds its own next cast, stack 011 ends with you at 80 life, stack 014 nets zero. Go. If it fails you kept the board — rebuild and go again.

Expect your quiet to be priced. "Combo decks look non-threatening while advancing in secret, which is exactly why experienced tables hit the quiet ramp player". So never claim you have nothing. Claim the board they can see — the swarm, the drains, the edicts — and sell that as the whole story.

Now the threat that runs the other way, and it is the one to respect. You do not fear a wrath; the drain layer makes a wrath a payment. You fear a two-mana artifact. A resolved Grafdigger's Cage or Cursed Totem covers four of your six kills on a straight oracle reading, and your 99 contains zero cards that destroy or exile an artifact — enumerated across all 80 entries, not estimated. Feed the Swarm destroys a creature or an enchantment an opponent controls, bills you its mana value in life, and touches nothing else. That card plus pressure is the entire out.

So play the artifact hole as politics, because that is what it is. Find the artifact-removal seats at Rule 0 — the green deck, the white deck — and invest in them early: your spot removal on their problems, Grave Pact's edicts on their attackers, your silence on their engines. The day a Cage resolves, their Disenchant is worth more to you than any card you draw. Overpay for it. Until then, pressure the hate player's life total so the lock piece is the card they never untap comfortably behind.

If it sticks anyway, do not shop for the answer you do not own. Pivot the same turn to the shapes that play through it on oracle reading — the Nest engine runs on freshly created tokens and Exquisite Blood never touches a graveyard — and aim the next tutor there. "Name your outs early — the removal or velocity that breaks the piece that beats *you*, not the scariest piece". Your outs are named: speed, and somebody else's artifact removal.

At Rule 0, say it straight. Bracket floor 4, forced by four Game Changers and by eleven two-card infinite records. Six rules-verified kill shapes, two unbounded, every one ending all opponents at the same moment. That last sentence is your go decision: you never present lethal to a seat, only to the table. So stop asking whether you can kill somebody. Ask whether what you hold is one of the six shapes, count the open mana, count the library, and go.

1
THREAT WINDOW: THE SHAPE, NOT THE SWARMThe table counts creatures. The goldfish has you at a mean of 4.239 bodies by turn six, which reads as a fair little swarm and is not the plan. The board that ends the game is two to four specific permanents, and nobody at a Commander table audits permanents for a shape. Let them count.
2
THREAT WINDOW: THE SECOND NONCREATURE PAYOFFThe flip is not board width — it is the second noncreature payoff resolving. Exquisite Blood. An Altar beside a Gravecrawler. Mikaeus untapping. Quiet combo decks are exactly who experienced tables hit, so never claim you have nothing: claim the board they can see — the swarm, the drains — and sell that as the whole story. Then sequence the loud half last and present at the end step before your own turn, with the table's open mana counted first.
3
THE PERMANENT YOU CANNOT BUY BACKGrafdigger's Cage sits on the graveyard-casting and undying halves of the suite on a straight oracle reading; Cursed Totem sits on the creature outlets and the commander himself. Nothing in all 80 entries destroys an artifact, so stop shopping for the answer you do not own. Find the artifact-removal seats at Rule 0, invest early — your spot removal on their problems, your silence on their engines — and the day a Cage resolves, their Disenchant is worth more than any card you draw. Overpay for it.
4
SAY IT STRAIGHT AT RULE ZEROBracket floor 4, Optimized, forced twice over: four Game Changers — Ancient Tomb, Demonic Tutor, Necropotence, Vampiric Tutor — and eleven two-card infinite records, inside a declared target of 4. Then the sentence that matters: six rules-verified kill shapes, two unbounded, every one ending all opponents at once. You never present lethal to a seat. Only to the table.
Pilot TipEquip at sorcery speed the turn before the engine turn — after the removal is announced it is decoration.
You get no warning turn, so neither does the table. Reveal the loud half at the end step before your own.
Table MannersMANA MAP · VOL. 008

Know Your Enemy

by Coach Sunny Brightside
★ COACHING
The decks that want you dead, and how to disappoint them.
THE SEATS ACROSS FROM YOU

WHO ACTUALLY BEATS YOU

It is not the wrath — the wrath pays you four different ways. It is a one-mana artifact. Read every box below for the same thing: which of the six shapes does this seat leave alive?

Sweeper controlThreat
Coach's read
★★★★★

Be visibly happy here. Nine drains bill the table when your creatures die, so their sweeper pays you for every body it takes, and your rebuild is structural: Reassembling Skeleton returns for two forever, Gravecrawler for one, Bitterblossom and Ophiomancer produce a body every upkeep without spending a card. Hold two mana whenever the control seat untaps with four or more, and let Plumb the Forbidden turn their announcement into your draw step. The real exposure sits one permanent type higher: every signed kill is a pile of permanents whose noncreature halves — Bastion, the Altar, the Blood — your three protection effects cannot save. Keep the noncreature half in hand until the turn it converts.

Your outs: Plumb the Forbidden, Reassembling Skeleton, Gravecrawler, Bitterblossom, Ophiomancer, Bastion of Remembrance, Victimize, Animate Dead, Phyrexian Reclamation

Cheap artifact hateThreat
Coach's read
★★★★★

This is the seat that actually beats you, and it costs them almost nothing. On a straight oracle reading a Grafdigger's Cage blanks the Gravecrawler recasts under both unbounded loops and every undying return; a Cursed Totem takes the commander's ability and every creature outlet you own. Nothing in the 99 answers either — so do not go shopping. Learn which shapes play through: the Nest engine runs on freshly created tokens, Exquisite Blood touches no graveyard, and under a Totem the noncreature outlets — both Altars and Blasting Station — are the remainder, unverified as a full assembly and honestly a lifeline rather than a plan. Pivot the same turn and aim the next tutor at the surviving shape.

Your outs: Nest of Scarabs, Exquisite Blood, South Wind Avatar, Marauding Blight-Priest, Starscape Cleric, Ashnod's Altar, Phyrexian Altar, Blasting Station

Stax and taxThreat
Coach's read
★★★★★

Recount your hand under a tax, never your board — this deck's best property there is verified, not vibes. The commander's ability costs no mana at all. File 009's Treasure exactly funds the next cast, file 011's Altar refunds its own black through a mana ability, file 012's loop is mandatory and spends nothing, file 014 nets zero life per cycle, and Jet Medallion rebates the first mana of every black spell before they even charge it. A Sphere halves everyone else's turn and does nothing to a loop already running. Your window of weakness is deployment, so deploy each piece the turn you can — the cleaner sequence is not coming.

Your outs: Phyrexian Altar, Ashnod's Altar, Blasting Station, Phyrexian Tower, Jet Medallion, Carrion Feeder

AggroThreat
Coach's read
★★★★★

You are the control seat and the sweepers need no apology: all three — Black Sun's Zenith, Soul Snuffers, The Meathook Massacre — double as engine ignition, because you are the one placing the counters, so no wipe slot is ever dead. An opposing small creature is not even a threat: the engine files establish that a two-toughness enemy body EXTENDS a Nest rotation by two activations, so point the commander's counters outward whenever a legal target exists and say thank you. Spend life freely early — one signed loop refunds you to 80 — and switch from spending to defending one turn before it matters, not one after.

Your outs: Black Sun's Zenith, Soul Snuffers, The Meathook Massacre, Grave Pact, Fatal Push, Gray Merchant of Asphodel, Ophiomancer

ComboThreat
Coach's read
★★★★★

Your hardest seat, so play it with your whole chest. You cannot interact on the stack at all — every answer you hold points at a permanent — so held answers rot: spend removal on the enabler the turn it lands. Deadly Rollick is free while your commander is out, which is most turns from four on. Then race with the shape you can finish, not the one you prefer: Exquisite Blood assembles earliest at a mean turn of 5.085 and hits 42.6% with a tutor behind it, and the commander's outlet works at instant speed once the permanents are down. One caution from the record: file 012's board ignited from South Wind Avatar's own dies-trigger lifegain — Marauding Blight-Priest and Starscape Cleric carry the drain text but gain no life themselves, so line up the first lifegain event before you count on either.

Your outs: Deadly Rollick, Fatal Push, Feed the Swarm, Bojuka Bog, Exquisite Blood

Graveyard hateThreat
Coach's read
★★★★★

It earns its own box because it splits your kill suite cleanly in half on a straight oracle reading: the Gravecrawler loops cast out of the graveyard every lap, and both counted lines return creatures through it — four of six shapes look Cage-shaped — while the Nest engine runs on freshly created tokens and Exquisite Blood never touches a graveyard at all. Nothing signed settles that split, so hold it as a working read: under hate, abandon recursion on the spot and tutor toward the engine (38.9% by turn six, assisted) or the Blood (fastest, mean 5.085). You hate back with one land: Bojuka Bog exiles a graveyard on an uncounted land drop.

Your outs: Nest of Scarabs, Exquisite Blood, South Wind Avatar, Marauding Blight-Priest, Starscape Cleric, Bojuka Bog

Assign your seat before your plan, and re-assign it every time the board turns over. Against fair decks you own the long game — act like it. Against anything assembling a lock or a clock faster than your assembly, you are the beatdown, and the polite value plan is how you lose.

THE FAIR, REMOVAL-HEAVY POD is your most common table and your best one. Grind it. Sacrifice on end steps, draw off every body, aim the counters at their creatures, and let Grave Pact turn each activation into an edict no other seat can sell. Mean bodies at turn six is 4.239 and the fodder and drain layers are each 82.3% by turn ten — the machine shows up almost every game, so the missing piece is the only thing worth spending a turn on.

Removal aimed at Yawgmoth here is a trade you accept with a smile: "protection slots and held mana cost you every game, removal only costs you some". Recast him, bank the goodwill, and save Lightning Greaves, Supernatural Stamina and Malakir Rebirth for the engine turn. Spend the tutors on kill halves — Nest of Scarabs first unless a faster shape is already half-built.

SWEEPER CONTROL is holding a wrath with your name on it. Invite it. Bastion of Remembrance, Zulaport Cutthroat and The Meathook Massacre each bill every opponent when a creature you control dies, and Blood Artist bills a chosen seat whenever any creature dies at all — so their sweeper pays you for every body it takes. "Assume the wrath — over a four-player game one is near-certain — so deploy in waves, hold a rebuild package" is your default stance anyway, and your rebuild package is structural: Reassembling Skeleton returns for two forever, Gravecrawler for one, Bitterblossom and Ophiomancer produce a body every upkeep without spending a card.

Hold two mana whenever a control seat untaps with four or more. Plumb the Forbidden turns their announcement into your draw step — sacrifice the board as the additional cost, take a copy per body, and every drain you already have down fires on the way through.

The real exposure sits one permanent type higher. Your kills are piles of permanents and the loops route through noncreature ones — Bastion of Remembrance sits in five verified boards, Exquisite Blood and Phyrexian Altar in others — while all three protection cards save only creatures. So keep the noncreature half of a configuration in hand until the turn it converts, and lead with the halves they will not spend answers on.

STAX AND TAX splits in two, and you must know which half you are sitting in. Against TAXES you are quietly the best-positioned deck at the table, and it is verified rather than vibes: the commander's ability costs no mana, stack 009's Treasure exactly funds the next cast, stack 011's Altar refunds its own black, stack 012 is mandatory and spends nothing, stack 014 nets zero per cycle, and Jet Medallion refunds a tax's first mana before they even charge it. A Sphere halves everyone else's turn and does nothing to a loop already running. Recount your hand, not your board, and deploy each piece the turn you can — the cleaner sequence is not coming.

Against LOCK PERMANENTS you are the worst deck at the table. No artifact answer exists in the 99, so your outs are speed and diplomacy, bought early, as the threat read above coaches. Under a Cursed Totem the creature outlets and the commander's own ability go dark; what remains is the noncreature outlets — Ashnod's Altar, Phyrexian Altar, Blasting Station — and no verified line yet settles what assembles through them alone, so treat that remainder as a lifeline, not a plan. "You rarely own the long game under an assembling lock, so you are the beatdown, racing the lock's completion". Race.

AGGRO thinks a wide board and your 40 life are pressure. They are feeding you. Your three sweepers — Black Sun's Zenith, Soul Snuffers, The Meathook Massacre — all ignite the engine on the way down: you are the one putting the -1/-1 counters on, so Nest of Scarabs pays you an Insect per creature swept, and the Massacre drains as your own bodies die into it. Cast the wipe that leaves you a board.

Spend life like the early points they are — "early points are cheap and late points are priceless". Stack 011 ends with you at 80 and stack 014 costs nothing net, so Necropotence, Black Market Connections and City of Brass are cheap now and priceless later. Switch from spending to defending one turn before it matters, not one after. And an opposing small creature is not even a threat: a two-toughness attacker extends a Nest rotation by two activations (stacks 002 and 003), so point the counters outward and say thank you.

COMBO is your worst seat, so play it with your whole chest. You cannot touch the stack — your instant-speed answers are Deadly Rollick and Fatal Push, both aimed at creatures — so spend them on the enabler the turn it lands. Rollick is free while your commander is out, which is most turns from four on. "Held answers rot — against combo, or lethal already on board, the last profitable moment is now".

Then race with the shape you can finish, not the one you prefer. Exquisite Blood is your fastest assembly at mean turn 5.085 and reaches 42.6% assembled with a tutor behind it, and the commander's outlet works at instant speed, so once the permanents are down the close does not wait for your main phase. One caution: stack 012's board ignites off South Wind Avatar's own death-trigger lifegain. Marauding Blight-Priest and Starscape Cleric carry the same drain text and gain no life themselves — line up the first lifegain event before you count on either.

GRAVEYARD HATE gets its own read because it splits your kill suite in half on a straight oracle reading. The Gravecrawler loops cast out of the graveyard every iteration and both undying lines return through it, so a resolved Cage effect or crypt-emptier looks lights-out for four shapes — while the Nest engine runs on freshly created tokens and Exquisite Blood never touches a graveyard at all. Nothing verified settles that split yet, so hold it as a working read: under hate, abandon recursion on the spot and tutor toward the Nest engine (38.9% by turn six, assisted) or Exquisite Blood (fastest, mean 5.085). "Know what you can't beat and stop paying for it".

You hate back with one land. Bojuka Bog exiles a graveyard on an uncounted land drop — hold it for the turn it strips a reanimation target, not the turn it is convenient. Against a graveyard deck it is the cheapest removal spell you own.

Pilot TipBank it — it enters tapped anyway, so play it on the turn their graveyard matters rather than the turn it is convenient.
Interaction slots are the price of a linear engine. You paid it, and the bill arrives as a two-mana artifact you cannot remove.
Know Your EnemyMANA MAP · VOL. 008

Fetch Quests

by Coach Sunny Brightside
★ COACHING
You get one wish per tutor. Here's how not to waste it.
FIVE WISHES

WISH FOR THE KILL, NOT THE ENGINE

Your enabler classes turn up on their own in four games of five. Your kills do not — so what is a tutor actually buying, and which single card completes two of them?

The table watches you cast a tutor and assumes you just found the kill. Let them assume it. Most wishes here buy the engine, and the engine looks like value right up until it is lethal.

Start with what arrives without any help, because it is nearly everything. By turn ten you are holding a drain 82.3% of the time, renewable fodder 82.3%, a free sacrifice outlet 78.5% and an ignition source 73.8% — so never spend a wish on a covered class. Those cards are already coming.

One class is not covered. Fast mana is three cards and 43.6% of games, the smallest and lowest-rated engine class on the sheet — the single non-kill errand a wish is allowed to run, and every entry below that runs it says so out loud.

Now the heart of the matter. The primary line — Yawgmoth from the zone, Nest of Scarabs, a drain, roughly two drains per body on board (stacks 002, 003 and 004) — is assembled by turn six in 7.5% of games on your own draws. A wish takes it to 38.9%. Nest is one card in ninety-nine, and these five tutors are the only redundancy it has, so Nest of Scarabs is the default fetch. Demand a reason to deviate.

Six kill shapes, every one signed by the checker. Learn them by name so you can say which one you are a card away from. KILL E is the Nest engine above. KILL C is Exquisite Blood plus any gain-and-drain body — the earliest assembler at a mean of turn 5.1 and the biggest mover on the sheet, 6.6% to 42.6% with a wish, and stack 012 says the loop is mandatory, not a draw. KILL A (stack 009) and KILL B (stack 011) are the Gravecrawler loops, unbounded, 18.2% and 12.0% assisted. KILL D (stack 010) and KILL F (stack 014) are the undying lines — lethal but library-bounded, because your commander's draw is mandatory. Stack 014 prices F at thirty activations against roughly twenty-five cards of margin, so count before you start, never during.

Two cards carry the loop kills, so let arithmetic pick the fetch. Gravecrawler turns up on your own draws 17.0% of the time by turn ten and completes both unbounded kills; Bastion of Remembrance at 17.8% is the payoff half of three signed lines. A wish on either is a wish on several kills at once.

Name the shape, then check its precondition. KILL A and KILL B cast Gravecrawler out of the graveyard, which needs a Zombie the loop never sacrifices — Warren Soultrader in 009, Carrion Feeder in 011. Fetch Gravecrawler with no second Zombie and you have bought a 2/1 that cannot block.

One wish is the emergency brake. Feed the Swarm is the only card in the ninety-nine that answers a noncreature permanent — 17.1% on your own draws, 67.1% when a wish buys it. When an enchantment locks your graveyard, that is the fetch, at any price.

Two limits. You draw a tutor at all in 61.2% of games by turn ten, so build every plan to work without one and treat the wish as the upgrade, not the plan. And there is no union kill rate anywhere on the sheet — each shape is priced alone. Quote the numbers separately and never add them.

Vampiric Tutor

1
Turn three, end step of the player before you, {B} open. Yawgmoth is the turn-four play — he lands there in 7,188 of 10,000 games — and no engine piece is on your battlefield.Fetch: Nest of Scarabs
The default fetch, and the whole reason this deck runs five tutors. The primary line is together by turn six in 7.5% of games on your own draws and 38.9% with a wish — Nest is the one-of every version of that engine routes through, so the wish IS the consistency. Two life at instant speed, the draw step delivers it, three mana casts it, and the commander walks into a running engine: stacks 002, 003 and 004 signed the shape at roughly two drains per body per activation.
2
Player 2 resolves Rest in Peace with your Gravecrawler already in the graveyard. Your board is a sacrifice outlet and two bodies, and it is Player 2's end step with {B} open.Fetch: Feed the Swarm
Stop the bleeding, because a graveyard lock is not an inconvenience here — two signed kills cast Gravecrawler out of the graveyard, so that enchantment deletes them both. Feed the Swarm is the only card among all eighty entries that answers a noncreature permanent: 17.1% on your own draws by turn ten, 67.1% when a wish buys it, which makes this wish most of your outs. Book it at their end step, cast it on your turn, pay the life without flinching.
3
End step of the player before you, turn seven. Warren Soultrader and Bastion of Remembrance are both on the battlefield, you have {B} untapped, and the table is spent — Player 2 tapped out for a six-drop, Player 4 down to two cards.Fetch: Gravecrawler
Take the cheapest complete kill on the board: one black at instant speed and two life. Draw it, cast it for {B}, and stack 009 runs — each sacrifice to Soultrader mints the Treasure that pays the next cast, your life stays net zero, and every seat goes down together. Soultrader is the Zombie that switches Gravecrawler on and the loop never touches him, because his cost reads sacrifice ANOTHER creature. Count the table's open mana before you start.
4
Turn one. One Swamp, {B} open, a second land in hand and no acceleration anywhere. Nobody has committed anything and nobody is watching you.Fetch: Dark Ritual
This wish exists on turn one and nowhere else, so take it while it is live. Ritual arrives on your turn-two draw: Swamp plus Ritual is three black, the second land is the fourth mana, and that is Yawgmoth on turn two — the sim records him there in 718 of 10,000 games. Fast mana is the one engine class the deck does not deliver on its own — three cards, 43.6% by turn ten — so acceleration is the one non-kill errand worth a wish. Own the judgment: the goldfish models no rituals, so this line sits above the sheet, not inside it.

Two life is never free in this deck. You are already paying Yawgmoth one per activation, plus Bitterblossom, Necropotence, Black Market Connections, City of Brass, Ancient Tomb, Cabal Pit and Ifnir Deadlands — so fire this while your total is comfortable and stop treating the cost as rounding. And fire it as the instant it is: the end step before your own turn is almost always the moment, because the transit turn then costs you nothing.

Demonic Tutor

1
Turn five, five mana after your land drop. Marauding Blight-Priest is on the battlefield and nobody has pointed removal at it — the table is reading you as a value deck.Fetch: Exquisite Blood
Buy the biggest mover on the sheet. KILL C runs 6.6% on your own draws and 42.6% with a wish, assembling earliest at a mean of turn 5.1 — and Exquisite Blood is the half only a to-hand tutor delivers, because at mana value 5 a bargained Beseech can never free-cast it and a top-of-library wish costs you the turn you need for the cast. Demonic does not care what the card costs; this is the wish that fits. Stack 012 signed the shape: the loop is mandatory, emphatically not a draw, and it takes every opponent to zero at once.
2
Turn eight, seven or more mana. Yawgmoth and Bastion of Remembrance are on the battlefield and you have two non-Human bodies you are happy to feed him — Viscera Seer and Gravecrawler, say.Fetch: Mikaeus, the Unhallowed
Reach for the card no other wish in this suite can reach — mana value 6 sits outside every cap you own. He turns your commander's own ability into KILL D: the -1/-1 counter annihilates undying's +1/+1 at the next check, the body comes back re-armed, and the pair alternates forever on life that stays flat (stack 010). Measured at 4.6% on your own draws and 34.3% with this wish. It is lethal but library-bounded — thirty-nine further activations zero a 40-life table, and the library is the hard bound the stack names — so count the whole kill before the first activation.
3
Your board is Yawgmoth, four creatures and a drain. Player 2 is the control seat, has left four or more mana untapped for two turns running, and declined a profitable attack. Assume a sweeper in that hand.Fetch: Plumb the Forbidden
Buy the rebuild instead of the restraint, and keep deploying. This wish only works from a to-hand tutor — an instant sitting on top of your library is an instant you do not have when the wrath resolves — which is exactly why it belongs on Demonic. Plumb turns the wave into cards: sacrifice your creatures in response, copy it once per body, draw one more than you sacrificed. With a drain already down, every one of those deaths is damage on the way past.
4
Butcher Ghoul and Geralf's Messenger are on the battlefield with Yawgmoth and Zulaport Cutthroat, a free outlet up, and KILL F is your clock. Three mana spare, and the count says thirty activations against twenty-five cards of margin.Fetch: Pawn of Ulamog
Shorten the count nobody else is counting. Stack 014 signed the bare undying pair as library-bounded — lethal at thirty activations, about twenty-five cards to spare — and Pawn banks an Eldrazi Spawn every time a nontoken creature of yours dies. In the cycle that is every single activation: each Spawn refunds {C}, and fed to your outlet it is one more drain trigger per activation, so the same library buys more damage. The Spawn arithmetic is your judgment stacked on a signed bound, not a signed board — say so if you announce it. Seven contained combo records also name Pawn beside Pitiless Plunderer and Reassembling Skeleton; none is signed, so fetch into those as value, never as a stated kill.

No condition, no delay, no life, no mana-value cap — so fire it at the first genuine hole rather than saving it for a cleverer turn that may not come. It is also the only one of the five to spend on a card costing five or more: what you pay is a turn of board impact, so make the card you name worth a turn.

Diabolic Intent

1
Turn five, five mana. Bastion of Remembrance is on the battlefield, Gravecrawler is in your graveyard, and you have three bodies including a token you were going to feed Yawgmoth anyway.Fetch: Warren Soultrader
Take three cards in one slot: the free repeatable outlet, the Treasure that funds each recast, and the Zombie that lets Gravecrawler cast from the graveyard. Fetch him and stack 009's board is complete — two mana for the wish and three for the body is exactly the five you have, so assemble in one turn. Pay Intent's cost with the token, never a piece; with Bastion out that sacrifice is already a drain trigger, and with Pawn of Ulamog out a nontoken body even refunds a Spawn. KILL A is 1.3% on your own draws and 18.2% with this wish — that jump is the whole reason to spend it here.
2
Gravecrawler, Carrion Feeder and Bastion of Remembrance are all on the battlefield. Five mana, Player 3 at 12 with four mana up, and you would rather not hand anyone a turn to find an answer.Fetch: Phyrexian Altar
Take the kill that costs you nothing at all — no life, no cards, no net mana, and your own total climbs while the table falls (stack 011). Carrion Feeder is the Zombie that stays and is never the sacrifice; the Altar's mana ability never uses the stack, so each recast is funded the instant Gravecrawler dies. Pay Intent's own cost with Gravecrawler if you like — he comes straight back for one black. Never pay it with Carrion Feeder.
3
The wrath resolved. You have one creature left, two cards in hand, five mana, and the table is rebuilding around you. Yawgmoth is castable but there is nothing to feed him.Fetch: Ophiomancer
Sacrifice the survivor — it was doing nothing alone — and buy a body every upkeep. Every upkeep, not every turn: in a four-player pod that is up to four deathtouch Snakes a round as long as you keep feeding them to Yawgmoth, and the one you leave standing trades with the biggest thing anyone rebuilds into. Renewable fodder is the class this engine spends fastest, and this is the version that defends while it feeds. Rebuild first and make them react to you.
4
The sweeper took the kill with it. Warren Soultrader and Gravecrawler are both in your graveyard, Bastion of Remembrance survived because it is an enchantment, and you have two small bodies left — a token and a Reassembling Skeleton you rebought. Five mana.Fetch: Victimize
Take the wish nobody sees coming, because it turns Intent's cost into the setup. Sacrifice the token to Intent, fetch Victimize, then let Victimize sacrifice the Skeleton and return TWO creature cards tapped — Soultrader and Gravecrawler both, off one card. Tapped costs you nothing here: neither needs to tap and Gravecrawler cannot block anyway. That is a whole kill rebuilt out of the graveyard for five mana, and the Skeleton buys itself back later.

Treat the additional cost as upside almost everywhere: with any drain out it is damage, with Pawn of Ulamog out a nontoken sacrifice refunds a Spawn, with Mikaeus out it is an undying return. It is a genuine tax in exactly three spots — when your only creature is the engine making the others, when you control no creature and the spell is uncastable, and on turn three, where the sim has you at a mean of 1.781 bodies. Read the board before you keep it in an opener.

Beseech the Mirror

1
Turn four, exactly four mana, a spare token to bargain — a Snake, an Eldrazi Spawn, a Treasure. Yawgmoth lands this turn or is already active, and no engine piece is down.Fetch: Nest of Scarabs
The default fetch, delivered: bargained, Beseech CASTS the card free at mana value 4 or less, and Nest is a 3. Four mana buys the search AND the engine in the same breath — no transit turn, no second cast, and the primary line moves from 7.5% assembled by turn six to 38.9% when a wish backs it. Keep the fuel honest: bargain the token the board replaces by itself, never the drain and never an engine piece.
2
Warren Soultrader and Bastion of Remembrance are already on the battlefield. You have exactly four mana and a spare token, and Player 4 is untapped with two blue sources open, so a counterspell is live and you want the whole line inside one turn.Fetch: Gravecrawler
Complete the shape you are already standing in, inside one spell. Mana value 1 sits comfortably under the free-cast cap, and the point is not the mana saved — Beseech exiles the card and casts it, so the loop starts the same turn with nothing else leaving your hand for them to answer. Nothing else fills this slot: Gravecrawler is the single card both unbounded kills route through, and you find him on your own only 17.0% of the time by turn ten.
3
Exquisite Blood resolved two turns ago and the table let it. You have four mana and a spare token to bargain, and the lowest life total at the table is 31.Fetch: South Wind Avatar
Know your own cap and fetch the half that fits under it. Exquisite Blood is mana value 5 and Beseech can never free-cast it; South Wind Avatar is exactly 4 and sits right on the line, with Marauding Blight-Priest at 3 and Starscape Cleric at 2 if you would rather keep mana up. Stack 012 signed this exact pair — mandatory, not a draw, every opponent to zero at once — so bargain the token, and remember that with a drain out the bargain itself is damage.
4
A Rest in Peace hit the table mid-game and your recursion kills went dark with it. Four mana, a spare token, and your turn.Fetch: Feed the Swarm
Bargain, search, and cast it free in the same breath — the lock is gone before you pass. Feed the Swarm is mana value 2, well under the cap, and it is the only noncreature answer in the ninety-nine: 17.1% on your own draws, 67.1% with a wish spent on it. The life you pay equals the enchantment's cost; pay it and take your graveyard back. A wish spent on defence is a wish not spent on a kill — spend it anyway, because half your kills live in that graveyard.

Two hard edges, both yours to check before you cast. The free cast caps at mana value 4: it reaches Nest of Scarabs, Bastion of Remembrance, Warren Soultrader, Phyrexian Altar, Marauding Blight-Priest, Starscape Cleric, Grave Pact, South Wind Avatar, Feed the Swarm and Jet Medallion, and it never reaches Exquisite Blood or Mikaeus. Never point the free cast at Black Sun's Zenith either — a cast without paying the cost sets X to zero and the wipe is a blank. Bargain fodder is a Treasure, a Snake, a Spawn or a spare token; sacrificing Bastion, Bitterblossom or Nest to find the next piece is the trap this card sets, so pick the fuel before the target. Unbargained it is a four-mana Demonic Tutor to hand — still fine, never the plan.

Insatiable Avarice

1
Turn eight, six mana. Warren Soultrader and Bastion of Remembrance are on the battlefield, your hand is empty, and Player 3 is the seat most likely to find an answer next turn.Fetch: Gravecrawler
Pay the full Spree and take the whole turn with it. The search mode resolves first and puts Gravecrawler on top; the draw mode — pointed at YOURSELF — takes delivery plus two more cards for 3 life. Cast him for one black and stack 009 runs the same turn. Six mana, two extra cards, and no turn handed to the table, where a top-only wish would have made you wait.
2
Mid-game grind. Yawgmoth has been answered once, so the next trip costs six, your hand holds three black spells, and you have three mana spare at sorcery speed.Fetch: Jet Medallion
Book the rebate. Three mana puts the Medallion on top, you draw and cast it for two, and every black spell for the rest of the game costs one less — the commander's taxed recast drops to five and the hand you are holding ships a turn earlier. Fast mana is the thinnest engine class on the sheet at 43.6%, and Dark Ritual's window closed on turn one; a standing cost cut is the acceleration a wish can still buy in the mid-game. Judgment, stated as such: the goldfish models no cost reducers, so this value never appears in a number.
3
Yawgmoth has been answered twice — the next trip from the zone costs eight. Your board holds Bitterblossom, Ayara and two drains, so your devotion to black is six. Three mana spare at sorcery speed.Fetch: Nykthos, Shrine to Nyx
The odd one nobody sees coming: a wish on a land. Book Nykthos, draw it, and it is your land drop — no cast, no window for anyone to answer — then two mana and a tap pay six black off that board, which is most of the recast from one land. Know what the sheet says and why: the colour-source count gives Nykthos no unconditional credit because its mana is conditional, and that is the count being conservative, not the land being bad. Your board is the credit — seventy-six black pips across fifty-five spells means devotion assembles itself here. Fetch it at devotion four or better; below that you have booked a colorless land.
4
Endgame. Player 3 is at 3 after paying for their own fetches and a Necropotence of their own, the other two seats are in the high teens, and you have five mana and a board of black permanents.Fetch: Gray Merchant of Asphodel
Nobody counts this one, so count it: cast the full Spree, let the search book Gray Merchant, and point the draw mode at PLAYER 3 — who draws three cards, loses 3 life, and is gone before the cards matter. Then the Merchant lands next turn for devotion in a list carrying seventy-six black pips across fifty-five spells. One seat removed by the tutor itself, the rest drained by what it fetched. Expect the coalition to form that turn and have your next two turns planned before you cast it.

Read it as two tutors and pick which one you are casting. At three mana it is a slower Demonic that lands on top and costs a turn — fine when you have a turn to spend, and the only sensible delivery for a land. At five it is the only card in the deck that fetches and delivers in one breath, and 3 life is the right price in a shell that gains one back per drain trigger. The mode order is in your favour: the search resolves before the draw, so pointing the draw at yourself always takes delivery of what you just booked.

1
NEVER WISH FOR A COVERED CLASSBy turn ten, on your own draws: a drain 82.3%, renewable fodder 82.3%, a free repeatable outlet 78.5%, an ignition source 73.8%. Those cards are already coming. The one engine errand a wish is allowed to run is fast mana — three cards, 43.6%, the smallest and lowest-rated class on the sheet.
2
THE KILLS ARE WHAT MOVESThe Nest engine goes 7.5% to 38.9% assembled by turn six. Exquisite Blood 6.6% to 42.6% overall — the biggest mover on the sheet. The Mikaeus line 4.6% to 34.3%, the undying pair 1.8% to 26.9%, the Soultrader loop 1.3% to 18.2%, the Altar loop 0.6% to 12.0%. Multiples, every one. That is the entire job of these five cards.
3
THE DEFAULT FETCH IS NEST OF SCARABSThe primary line runs through one card with no substitute in the colour, and these five tutors are its only redundancy — demand a reason to deviate. When you do: Gravecrawler completes both unbounded kills at once (17.0% on your own draws), and Bastion of Remembrance is the drain five signed files resolved with (17.8%). A wish on either is a wish on several kills.
4
KNOW YOUR OWN CAPSBeseech the Mirror's bargained free cast stops at mana value 4: it reaches Nest of Scarabs, Bastion of Remembrance, Warren Soultrader, Phyrexian Altar, Marauding Blight-Priest, Starscape Cleric, South Wind Avatar and Feed the Swarm — and never Exquisite Blood at 5 or Mikaeus at 6. Those two belong to Demonic Tutor. Vampiric Tutor lands on top and costs the draw step, so book it at the end step before your own turn.
Pilot TipCast both Spree modes and point the draw at yourself — the search resolves first, so the draw delivers exactly what you just booked.
You are not tutoring to make this deck function. You are tutoring to convert.
Fetch QuestsMANA MAP · VOL. 008

Sources Say

by "Ledger" Lin Marginal
◆ DATA-DERIVED
Pips versus sources — does this mana base keep its promises?
PIPS VERSUS SOURCES

ONE COLOUR, NOTHING TO RECONCILE

Pip share and source share cannot disagree when there is only one colour to be short of, so the audit narrows to a single number — and the yardstick it is measured against is not reachable in a deck built like this one. Here is what the gap actually costs: almost nothing.

Thirty-six land copies across 16 entries, and only three enter tapped — two of those always. Twenty-one are basic Swamps, so well over half the base is a single card repeated, and that concentration is exactly why Cabal Coffers counts as one source on paper and behaves like something else entirely at the table.

Pip share and source share both read 1.0. They agree perfectly because there is only one colour here to disagree about, so there is nothing to reconcile — just a count. 33 black land sources against a target of 36, and the whole gap is the three lands that make no counted black at all: Ancient Tomb taps for {C}{C}, Urborg taps for nothing on its own, and Nykthos gets no unconditional credit because its devotion payout is exactly the kind of restriction the counter refuses to price. The analysis puts the caveat plainly, and it is worth quoting: "the target is a yardstick, not a possibility, in a 36-land deck".

Nonland producers carry 33 up to 41, and the composition matters far more than the total. Ten cards produce; eight of them add black, and not one of the eight does it unconditionally — the rituals are one-shots or the back faces of lands, and Phyrexian Altar, Pitiless Plunderer, Pawn of Ulamog and Warren Soultrader all want a creature to die first. In this deck that is less credit than it sounds like: creatures die here on schedule. Jet Medallion never shows up in the source column at all, because a discount is not a source — it is one generic off every black spell, which is a different way of being an extra land.

The demand side is mild for a mono-colour deck. 76 black pips across 55 cards, a maximum of 3 on any one card and an effective 2, with the first pip due on turn 1. Double black on curve is what this base is actually asked for, and 33 land sources is comfortably more than that costs: 86.9% on-curve from lands alone, 94.8% once the rocks and dorks are counted — call it eight points bought on credit, with no survival discount taken on the creatures underwriting it.

Development agrees. The land drop hits 99.7% on turn 2, 91.6% on turn 3 and 80.7% on turn 4, with mean available mana of 3.079, 3.908 and 5.230 on turns 3, 4 and 6 against a commander who costs four and lands on a median turn 4. Nothing here is ever stuck on colour. The mana is not the number in this deck worth worrying about.

What the audit flags

  • B: 33 land sources against the 36 a 90% on-curve rate would take — the target is a yardstick, not a possibility, in a 36-land deck; rocks and dorks lift the count to 41.
What this audit does and does not do. Hypergeometric draws, not games. Every assumption is stated:
  • Colour probabilities are hypergeometric draws from a 99-card library — no mulligans, no card selection, no fetching decisions.
  • A source counts only for mana it produces unconditionally; restricted mana (“spend this mana only…”) counts for nothing (the deck-builder's rule).
  • Hybrid pips charge half a pip to each side.
  • Rocks and dorks are counted as sources from the turn they can tap, with no survival discount on creatures.
1
THE YARDSTICK YOU CANNOT HITThe model wants 36 black sources for a 90% on-curve rate. The lands supply 33 — the whole gap is the three that make no counted black: Ancient Tomb taps for {C}{C}, Urborg taps for nothing on its own, and Nykthos gets no unconditional credit because devotion is exactly the restriction the counter refuses to price. Lands alone: 86.9% on curve. The audit says it plainly: the target is a yardstick, not a possibility, in a 36-land deck.
2
EIGHT PRODUCERS CLOSE ITTen nonland cards produce mana and eight add black — Agadeem's Awakening, Dark Ritual, Deadly Dispute, Malakir Rebirth, Pawn of Ulamog, Phyrexian Altar, Pitiless Plunderer, Warren Soultrader — lifting 33 to 41 sources and on-curve black to 94.8%. Not one of the eight does it unconditionally: rituals are one-shots, and the rest want a creature to die first. In this deck, that is less of a caveat than it sounds. Restricted mana anywhere in the 99: none.
3
ENGINE MANA IS NOT FAST MANAFive cards turn a creature death into castable black — 61.2% by turn ten — and that is what makes a sacrifice loop mana-positive rather than merely free. Fast mana is a different resource: Sol Ring, Ancient Tomb, Dark Ritual, 43.6%, the class that buys the turn-two commander. The deck's mana is engine-shaped, not acceleration-shaped, and the two classes buy different turns.
4
THREE LANDS THE COUNT TREATS ODDLYUrborg produces nothing and counts for zero sources while making every land on the battlefield a Swamp. Cabal Coffers counts as exactly one source and taps for {B} per Swamp, beside 21 basics. Nykthos counts for zero and pays your devotion to black in a list carrying 76 black pips. One source, zero, and zero is the accounting. It is not the experience.
Pilot TipPlay it before Cabal Coffers, not after — every land you already control becomes a Swamp the Coffers can count.
Seventy-six black pips across 55 cards, at most three in one cost — and the first is demanded on turn one. Development answers: 99.7% on-time on turn two, 91.6% on turn three.

THE ENGINE ARRIVES. THE KILL IS FETCHED.

Ten thousand openings, seed 42, no opponents and no interaction. The gap between the enabler bars and the kill bars is this deck's whole personality — and there is one number this page refuses to print.

Simulation File

Decklist sha

What this model does and does not do. These runs simulate resource development, not full games. Every assumption is stated:
  • Simulates resource development, not full games (no interaction, no removal).
  • Draw every turn including turn 1 (multiplayer Commander).
  • Mulligan: keep 7-card hands with 2-5 lands; up to 2 fresh redraws, keep the last.
  • One land drop per turn when available.
  • Mana rocks ('{T}: Add') contribute from the turn after they are cast.
  • Commander cast on first affordable turn (highest spending priority).
  • Bodies count = creatures cast + tokens parsed from 'create ... token' text.
  • Target assembly counts cards DRAWN by a turn (cast cards still count).
  • Unrestricted tutors ('search your library for a card') are modeled as wildcards: a tutor that has been drawn and is affordable fills ONE missing any_of group. Consumed once, mana paid, and a tutor that puts the card on top of the library costs a turn. Reported as the *_assisted figures; the unassisted figures beside them exclude tutors entirely.
  • Cost reducers, rituals, and extra card draw are not modeled (conservative).
1
READ EVERY BAR AS A PAIRThe first figure excludes tutors entirely; the second lets one drawn, affordable tutor fill a single missing group. By turn ten: a drain 82.3% / 93.6%. Renewable fodder 82.3% / 93.3%. A free repeatable outlet 78.5% / 92.5%. An ignition source 73.8% / 90.2%. Engine mana 61.2% / 85.8%. A tutor itself: 61.2%, and it cannot assist its own arrival.
2
SIX KILLS, SIX NUMBERSThe Nest engine 13.5% / 58.8% assembled, mean turn 6.267 — the deck's best-measured line by a wide margin. Exquisite Blood 6.6% / 42.6%, and fastest at a mean of 5.085. Mikaeus 4.6% / 34.3%. The undying pair 1.8% / 26.9%. The Soultrader loop 1.3% / 18.2%. The Altar loop 0.6% / 12.0%. Several rest on one named card, and a named card is about 7% of an opening seven.
3
THE NUMBER THAT DOES NOT EXISTThere is no union. The simulator scores each requirement group on its own, so a hand holding Exquisite Blood, Gravecrawler and Zulaport Cutthroat satisfies three groups while assembling no kill at all. Nothing on this sheet means 'any kill assembled', we will not compute one, and you should not add these six together either — the shapes share pieces, so the sum double-counts.
4
THE COMMANDER'S HISTOGRAMTurn four in 7,188 games of 10,000, mean 4.301, median 4, on the battlefield by turn six in 91%. Turn two happens 718 times, every one off the three-card fast-mana class. He never arrives at all in 150.
5
THE BAR THAT NAMES THE PROBLEMAn answer to a noncreature permanent, drawn by turn ten: 17.1% — one card, Feed the Swarm, reaching creature or enchantment only, and 67.1% costs a wish that was supposed to buy a kill half. Every other bar measures how well the engine turns up. This one measures what happens when somebody turns it off.
6
WHAT THE SIMULATION IS NOTIt models resource development, not games: no opponents, no removal, no politics, and a group counts as assembled the moment its cards are drawn. Cost reducers, rituals and extra card draw are not modeled — which means the commander's own mandatory draw, the deck's real selection engine, sits outside every rate quoted. Read the unassisted figures as floors, never predictions.
Six kills. Six numbers. Adding them up would be the only lie in this issue.
